# Daily Summary: 2025-04-27

### IberBench: LLM Evaluation on Iberian Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16921v1)
- **Authors**: José Ángel González, Ian Borrego Obrador, Álvaro Romo Herrero, Areg Mikael Sarvazyan, Mara Chinea-Ríos, Angelo Basile, Marc Franco-Salvador
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) remain difficult to evaluate comprehensively, particularly for languages other than English, where high-quality data is often limited. Existing benchmarks and leaderboards are predominantly English-centric, with only a few addressing other languages. These benchmarks fall short in several key areas: they overlook the diversity of language varieties, prioritize fundamental Natural Language Processing (NLP) capabilities over tasks of industrial relevance, and are static. With these aspects in mind, we present IberBench, a comprehensive and extensible benchmark designed to assess LLM performance on both fundamental and industry-relevant NLP tasks, in languages spoken across the Iberian Peninsula and Ibero-America. IberBench integrates 101 datasets from evaluation campaigns and recent benchmarks, covering 22 task categories such as sentiment and emotion analysis, toxicity detection, and summarization. The benchmark addresses key limitations in current evaluation practices, such as the lack of linguistic diversity and static evaluation setups by enabling continual updates and community-driven model and dataset submissions moderated by a committee of experts. We evaluate 23 LLMs ranging from 100 million to 14 billion parameters and provide empirical insights into their strengths and limitations. Our findings indicate that (i) LLMs perform worse on industry-relevant tasks than in fundamental ones, (ii) performance is on average lower for Galician and Basque, (iii) some tasks show results close to random, and (iv) in other tasks LLMs perform above random but below shared task systems. IberBench offers open-source implementations for the entire evaluation pipeline, including dataset normalization and hosting, incremental evaluation of LLMs, and a publicly accessible leaderboard.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the IberBench paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces IberBench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs) in Iberian languages (Spanish, Portuguese, Catalan, Basque, and Galician, along with various Spanish dialects).  The benchmark addresses limitations of existing evaluations, which are typically English-centric, prioritize fundamental NLP tasks, and are static. IberBench includes 101 datasets spanning 22 task categories, integrates datasets from existing evaluation campaigns and recent benchmarks, and enables continual updates and community-driven submissions.  The authors evaluate 23 LLMs ranging from 100 million to 14 billion parameters and present findings indicating performance disparities across tasks, languages, and model types. The IberBench evaluation pipeline, including dataset normalization and hosting, is open-sourced, along with a public leaderboard.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the combination of several aspects:  (1) Focus on Iberian languages and dialects, an underrepresented area in LLM evaluation. (2) Integration of a large number of datasets, especially drawing from Iberian workshops (IberLEF, IberEval, TASS, PAN), making previously scattered and difficult-to-access datasets readily available. (3) Emphasizing both fundamental NLP *and* industry-relevant tasks, addressing a bias in existing benchmarks. (4) The establishment of a continual, community-driven benchmark with expert moderation, addressing the static nature of many existing resources. While individual aspects aren't entirely new (e.g., other smaller Iberian benchmarks exist), the scale and comprehensive nature of IberBench, along with its focus on sustained relevance, represents a significant advance. The sequence labeling evaluation integrated into lm-evaluation-harness is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance is considerable. IberBench fills a clear gap in LLM evaluation by providing a valuable resource for assessing performance in Iberian languages.  The open-source nature of the evaluation pipeline and the public leaderboard will facilitate further research and development in this area.  The benchmark’s design promotes continuous improvement through community contributions, ensuring its longevity and relevance.  The findings regarding the performance disparities across tasks and languages provide actionable insights for future LLM development and adaptation for the Iberian context. The paper's insights regarding underperformance in sequence-labeling tasks, relative to shared-task system performance, and the observation that certain smaller models perform below random baselines are important points for LLM developers.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive benchmark design, covering diverse tasks and languages.
    *   Open-source implementation and public leaderboard promotes transparency and collaboration.
    *   Community-driven approach with expert moderation ensures relevance and sustainability.
    *   Empirical analysis provides valuable insights into LLM performance in Iberian languages.
    *   Addresses crucial limitations of existing LLM evaluation practices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on existing datasets means IberBench inherits any biases present in those resources. While the authors acknowledge and mitigate contamination risks, it is still a limitation.
    *   The evaluation is primarily zero-shot, potentially underestimating the capabilities of some LLMs. Though the authors justify this decision, exploring few-shot performance in future work could yield additional insights.
    *   The language coverage, while improved, is still not perfectly balanced. Some dialects and languages are underrepresented due to data scarcity. This could impact the generalizability of some of the conclusions.
    *   The reliance on ROUGE-1 for text summarization is a known limitation due to its simplicity, and may not capture the nuanced qualities of LLM generated summaries.

*   **Potential Influence:**  IberBench has the potential to become a standard benchmark for LLM evaluation in Iberian languages, driving research and development in this area. It will likely inform the development of more effective and equitable LLMs for Iberian language speakers. It may also serve as a template for the development of similar benchmarks for other under-resourced language families.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of 8 because the paper addresses a significant gap in LLM evaluation and provides a valuable, well-designed, and open-source resource for the community. The comprehensive nature of the benchmark, its focus on sustained relevance through community involvement, and its empirical findings contribute significantly to the field. The identified weaknesses related to dataset biases, zero-shot evaluation, and reliance on a simple summarization metric, prevent it from achieving a higher score. The high score (i.e. greater than 5) is based on the justification that this is a strong and significant paper, with the potential to make a broad impact on the field of NLP (despite the weakenesses discussed).

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Safety Pretraining: Toward the Next Generation of Safe AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16980v1)
- **Authors**: Pratyush Maini, Sachin Goyal, Dylan Sam, Alex Robey, Yash Savani, Yiding Jiang, Andy Zou, Zacharcy C. Lipton, J. Zico Kolter
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in high-stakes settings, the risk of generating harmful or toxic content remains a central challenge. Post-hoc alignment methods are brittle: once unsafe patterns are learned during pretraining, they are hard to remove. We present a data-centric pretraining framework that builds safety into the model from the start. Our contributions include: (i) a safety classifier trained on 10,000 GPT-4 labeled examples, used to filter 600B tokens; (ii) the largest synthetic safety dataset to date (100B tokens) generated via recontextualization of harmful web data; (iii) RefuseWeb and Moral Education datasets that convert harmful prompts into refusal dialogues and web-style educational material; (iv) Harmfulness-Tag annotations injected during pretraining to flag unsafe content and steer away inference from harmful generations; and (v) safety evaluations measuring base model behavior before instruction tuning. Our safety-pretrained models reduce attack success rates from 38.8% to 8.4% with no performance degradation on standard LLM safety benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Safety Pretraining: Toward the Next Generation of Safe AI":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical challenge of ensuring safety in large language models (LLMs). Instead of solely relying on post-hoc alignment methods, which are often brittle and superficial, the authors propose a data-centric pretraining framework that embeds safety directly into the model from the start. Their approach involves several key components:

1.  **Safety Filtering:** A robust safety classifier, trained on GPT-4 labeled data, is used to filter harmful content from the pretraining corpus. The authors provide a Data Safety Report Card standard.

2.  **Synthetic Recontextualization:** Potentially harmful content is rephrased and reframed to preserve valuable information while embedding it in a context that underscores its ethical and historical implications. This results in a 100B token "SafeWeb" corpus.

3.  **Native Refusal Training:** The "RefuseWeb" dataset simulates refusal scenarios, guiding models to responsibly disengage from harmful prompts. The "Moral Education" dataset generalizes ethical patterns beyond dialogue.

4.  **Harmfulness-Tag Annotated Training:**  A harmfulness-tag is inserted into training data to signal potentially harmful passages, allowing LLMs to better separate safe and unsafe content. This tag is leveraged at inference time via a "SafeBeam" search algorithm.

5.  **New Evaluation Tools:** The paper presents novel evaluation tools to measure safety under 'completions' enabling the monitoring of safety during pre-training.

The authors evaluate their approach with a 1.7B parameter "SafeLM," demonstrating a significant reduction in attack success rates (ASR) from 38.8% to 8.3% on safety benchmarks without sacrificing performance on standard LLM benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core innovation lies in its comprehensive, data-centric approach to safety **during pretraining**. While individual components such as safety filtering or rephrasing are not entirely new, their synergistic combination, along with the introduction of harmfulness-tag annotation and SafeBeam inference, present a novel and significant advance over prior work.  Specifically, the work builds upon existing literature on pre-training safety interventions and extends the idea of safety annotations to the pre-training phase, which is a natural and valuable extension. The establishment of a Data Safety Report Card is a welcome and needed standardization practice.

*   **Significance:** The increasing deployment of LLMs in high-stakes settings makes safety a paramount concern. The paper's focus on natively safe models, rather than relying solely on alignment, addresses a key weakness in current approaches. The reported reduction in attack success rates, along with the preservation of performance on standard benchmarks, demonstrates the practical utility of their framework. Furthermore, the release of the SafeLM model, datasets, and evaluation tools promotes further research and development in the field. The exploration of the brittleness of post-training safety measures and their failure under benign fine-tuning adds significant weight to the argument for pretraining interventions.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The integration of multiple data-centric interventions strengthens the model's inherent safety.
    *   **Data-Driven:** The reliance on GPT-4 annotated data and synthetic datasets provides a scalable and adaptable approach to safety.
    *   **Effective Mitigation:** The results demonstrate a substantial reduction in attack success rates compared to baseline models.
    *   **Resource Release:** The open-source release of the SafeLM model, datasets, and evaluation tools promotes reproducibility and further research.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of post-hoc alignment methods and motivates the need for pretraining-based safety interventions.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The synthetic data generation and annotation process can be computationally expensive and may require specialized resources. The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the scaling properties of each component.
    *   **Model Size:** The SafeLM model, while effective, is relatively small (1.7B parameters) compared to state-of-the-art LLMs. The scalability of the proposed framework to larger models needs further investigation.
    *   **Subjectivity:** The assessment of harmfulness is inherently subjective, and the reliance on GPT-4 for annotation introduces potential biases.
    *   **Overrefusal:** While the paper argues overrefusal isn't a major cost, there is a slight drop in helpfulness that requires some acknowledgement and perhaps some focus on the best mechanisms for balancing safety and helpfulness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of safer and more responsible AI systems. The data-centric pretraining framework provides a practical and effective approach for embedding safety directly into LLMs. The release of the SafeLM model and associated resources could accelerate research and development in the field. The identified brittleness of surface-level alignment methods emphasizes the importance of native safety during pretraining.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**
The paper presents a significant advancement in LLM safety by focusing on pretraining interventions, validated by compelling empirical results. While there is potential for improvement, the novelty and importance of addressing LLM safety early in development warrant recognition.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### (Im)possibility of Automated Hallucination Detection in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17004v1)
- **Authors**: Amin Karbasi, Omar Montasser, John Sous, Grigoris Velegkas
- **Abstract**: Is automated hallucination detection possible? In this work, we introduce a theoretical framework to analyze the feasibility of automatically detecting hallucinations produced by large language models (LLMs). Inspired by the classical Gold-Angluin framework for language identification and its recent adaptation to language generation by Kleinberg and Mullainathan, we investigate whether an algorithm, trained on examples drawn from an unknown target language $K$ (selected from a countable collection) and given access to an LLM, can reliably determine whether the LLM's outputs are correct or constitute hallucinations. First, we establish an equivalence between hallucination detection and the classical task of language identification. We prove that any hallucination detection method can be converted into a language identification method, and conversely, algorithms solving language identification can be adapted for hallucination detection. Given the inherent difficulty of language identification, this implies that hallucination detection is fundamentally impossible for most language collections if the detector is trained using only correct examples from the target language. Second, we show that the use of expert-labeled feedback, i.e., training the detector with both positive examples (correct statements) and negative examples (explicitly labeled incorrect statements), dramatically changes this conclusion. Under this enriched training regime, automated hallucination detection becomes possible for all countable language collections. These results highlight the essential role of expert-labeled examples in training hallucination detectors and provide theoretical support for feedback-based methods, such as reinforcement learning with human feedback (RLHF), which have proven critical for reliable LLM deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a theoretical framework for analyzing the possibility of automated hallucination detection in Large Language Models (LLMs).  Inspired by Gold-Angluin's work on language identification, the authors model hallucination detection as a game between a learner and an adversary.  The adversary chooses a target language (representing correct statements) and the LLM's outputs.  The learner must determine if the LLM hallucinates (produces outputs outside the target language). The paper's main results are: (1) Hallucination detection is as hard as language identification when trained only on positive examples (correct statements).  (2) Hallucination detection becomes possible with expert-labeled feedback, including both positive and negative examples.  This emphasizes the importance of feedback-based methodologies like Reinforcement Learning with Human Feedback (RLHF).

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies primarily in adapting the classical Gold-Angluin framework to the problem of hallucination detection in LLMs. While the framework itself is well-established, the application to this specific problem is novel.  The connection between hallucination detection and language identification, along with the formal proof of their equivalence under certain conditions, is also a new contribution.  Prior theoretical work has explored the limitations of LLMs, but this paper provides a specific and rigorous connection to established learning theory. However, there is also research exploring related concepts, notably the hallucination-breadth trade-off using density of languages.

**Significance:**  The significance of the work stems from providing a theoretical foundation for empirical observations about hallucination detection.  The paper formalizes the intuitive idea that detecting incorrectness is difficult without explicitly knowing what is incorrect.  The result highlighting the necessity of negative examples (expert feedback) provides a theoretical justification for RLHF and other feedback-based approaches.  By demonstrating fundamental limits, the paper can help guide future research towards more promising directions, such as incorporating negative examples or exploring alternative detection strategies.  It can also help set realistic expectations for what can be achieved through automated hallucination detection.  The insights gained from this theoretical analysis have the potential to inform the design and training of more reliable and safe LLMs. However, theoretical results are just one component of impact, and the ability of these results to directly translate into improvements to empirical work needs to be proven.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Formalization:** The paper provides a clear and well-defined mathematical framework.
*   **Connection to Classical Theory:** The use of the Gold-Angluin framework grounds the analysis in established learning theory.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** The results provide a theoretical basis for empirical findings in hallucination detection.
*   **Clear Interpretation:** The paper clearly explains the implications of the theoretical results for practical LLM development.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Abstraction:** The model is highly abstract and simplified. Some real-world aspects of hallucination, like context dependence and subjectivity, are not fully captured.
*   **Limited Scope:** The focus is primarily on positive vs. negative examples, potentially overlooking other important aspects of hallucination detection.
*   **Practical Applicability:** While providing theoretical insights, the paper's immediate impact on practical LLM development might be limited. The theoretical results may not easily translate into concrete algorithmic improvements.
*   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The paper lacks empirical validation to connect the theoretical findings to real-world scenarios directly. While the theoretical setting is not fully representative of what exists in practice, it would be interesting to consider how it approximates it.

**Score:** 6

**Rationale:**

The paper introduces a theoretically sound framework to analyze the problem of hallucination detection in LLMs. The connection to classical language identification and the formal proof of the limitations of positive-example-only training are valuable contributions. However, the abstraction of the model limits its direct applicability and immediate impact. While the theoretical justification for using negative examples is significant, empirical work has already pointed towards this approach. The paper provides a formal framework for the empirical observation, which is important but not groundbreaking. While the results are insightful, the limited scope and the need for empirical validation justify a more moderate score. The paper has the potential to guide future research and inform the development of more robust hallucination detection methods, but its long-term influence is yet to be determined.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### LLM impact on BLV programming
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17018v1)
- **Authors**: Prashant Chandrasekar, Mariel Couvillion, Ayshwarya Saktheeswaran, Jessica Zeitz
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are rapidly becoming integral to a wide range of tools, tasks, and problem-solving processes, especially in software development. Originally designed for natural language processing tasks such as text generation, LLMs are increasingly being used to assist both professionals and students in writing code. This growing reliance on LLM-based tools is reshaping programming workflows and task execution. In this study, we explore the impact of these technologies on blind and low-vision (BLV) developers. Our review of existing literature indicates that while LLMs help mitigate some of the challenges faced by BLV programmers, they also introduce new forms of inaccessibility. We conducted an evaluation of five popular LLM-powered integrated development environments (IDEs), assessing their performance across a comprehensive set of programming tasks. Our findings highlight several unsupported scenarios, instances of incorrect model output, and notable limitations in interaction support for specific tasks. Through observing BLV developers as they engaged in coding activities, we uncovered key interaction barriers that go beyond model accuracy or code generation quality. This paper outlines the challenges and corresponding opportunities for improving accessibility in the context of generative AI-assisted programming. Addressing these issues can meaningfully enhance the programming experience for BLV developers. As the generative AI revolution continues to unfold, it must also address the unique burdens faced by this community.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of Large Language Models (LLMs) on Blind and Low-Vision (BLV) programmers. It begins with a literature review highlighting both the potential benefits LLMs offer (e.g., code summarization, error identification, targeted assistance) and the new challenges they introduce (e.g., hallucinations, accessibility issues of the LLM tools themselves). The paper then presents an evaluation of five popular LLM-powered Integrated Development Environments (IDEs), assessing their performance across a range of programming tasks relevant to BLV developers. The evaluation reveals inconsistencies in model outputs and limitations in interaction support.  Finally, it describes a user study where BLV developers engage in coding activities using LLMs. The study uncovers interaction barriers beyond model accuracy, such as difficulties in testing program behavior, over-reliance on potentially incorrect suggestions, and a tendency to overlook code comments. The paper concludes by outlining opportunities to improve accessibility in generative AI-assisted programming and emphasizing the importance of addressing the specific needs of the BLV community in the ongoing AI revolution.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the idea of using LLMs to assist developers, including those with disabilities, isn't entirely new, this paper differentiates itself by specifically focusing on the challenges and opportunities presented to BLV developers. The empirical approach of evaluating specific LLM-powered IDEs and conducting a user study adds practical value, going beyond theoretical discussions. However, the study has a small sample size (five participants) and only explores two specific programs.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial area of concern: ensuring equitable access to emerging technologies for individuals with disabilities. By identifying specific interaction barriers faced by BLV programmers when using LLMs, the paper provides valuable insights for developers of both LLM-based tools and assistive technologies. This can lead to more accessible and inclusive programming environments. The paper highlights the importance of incorporating accessibility considerations early in the design process of generative AI tools.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Focus on a specific user group:** The paper's targeted focus on BLV developers makes it more impactful than broader accessibility studies.
    *   **Empirical evaluation:** The combination of IDE evaluation and user study provides a solid foundation for the findings.
    *   **Actionable insights:** The paper identifies specific areas for improvement in LLM tools and development workflows.
    *   **Well-structured and clearly written:** The paper is easy to follow and presents its findings in a logical manner.
    *   **Addresses a gap:** It fills a gap in the literature by specifically considering how LLMs may impact accessibility for a specific group of developers (BLV developers).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Small Sample Size:** The user study's limited number of participants (five) restricts the generalizability of the findings. It is possible that the experiences of these individuals are not reflective of the larger BLV programmer population.
    *   **Limited Task Scope:** The study utilized two specific Python programs, which may not encompass the breadth of programming tasks and challenges faced by BLV developers in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Lack of Control Group:** The absence of a control group (BLV developers coding without LLM assistance) makes it difficult to definitively attribute observed changes in behavior and performance to LLM use. Without a direct comparison, it is challenging to quantify the true impact of LLMs on the coding experience of BLV developers.
    *   **Limited Generalizability** The tools and code tested were largely on Python IDEs. Given the ubiquity of other languages and tools, it can be more limited in its value.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design and development of more accessible LLM-based programming tools. It can also inform the training of AI models to better understand and respond to the needs of BLV developers. The research encourages greater awareness of accessibility issues in the AI community.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Assigned Score:** The paper deserves a solid score due to its relevant and timely focus on accessibility challenges in the context of generative AI, specifically for BLV developers. The combination of the LLM IDE testing, user feedback and analysis provides evidence to support the challenges posed by LLMs and GenAI which adds value to the research. Despite the small sample size and limited tasks the work highlights issues and provides potential areas for improvement in the design of accessible coding environments. These actions lead to positive support for a score assignment.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Optimizing LLMs for Italian: Reducing Token Fertility and Enhancing Efficiency Through Vocabulary Adaptation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17025v1)
- **Authors**: Luca Moroni, Giovanni Puccetti, Pere-Lluis Huguet Cabot, Andrei Stefan Bejgu, Edoardo Barba, Alessio Miaschi, Felice Dell'Orletta, Andrea Esuli, Roberto Navigli
- **Abstract**: The number of pretrained Large Language Models (LLMs) is increasing steadily, though the majority are designed predominantly for the English language. While state-of-the-art LLMs can handle other languages, due to language contamination or some degree of multilingual pretraining data, they are not optimized for non-English languages, leading to inefficient encoding (high token "fertility") and slower inference speed. In this work, we thoroughly compare a variety of vocabulary adaptation techniques for optimizing English LLMs for the Italian language, and put forward Semantic Alignment Vocabulary Adaptation (SAVA), a novel method that leverages neural mapping for vocabulary substitution. SAVA achieves competitive performance across multiple downstream tasks, enhancing grounded alignment strategies. We adapt two LLMs: Mistral-7b-v0.1, reducing token fertility by 25\%, and Llama-3.1-8B, optimizing the vocabulary and reducing the number of parameters by 1 billion. We show that, following the adaptation of the vocabulary, these models can recover their performance with a relatively limited stage of continual training on the target language. Finally, we test the capabilities of the adapted models on various multi-choice and generative tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates methods to adapt English-centric Large Language Models (LLMs) to Italian.  The authors focus on vocabulary adaptation to reduce token fertility (the average number of tokens a word is split into), leading to more efficient encoding and faster inference. They introduce a novel method called Semantic Alignment Vocabulary Adaptation (SAVA) that utilizes neural mapping for vocabulary substitution.  They apply SAVA and other adaptation techniques to Mistral-7B-v0.1 and Llama-3.1-8B, demonstrating reduced token fertility, competitive downstream task performance after continual training on Italian data, and parameter reduction in Llama-3.1-8B.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The introduction of SAVA is a key element of novelty.  While vocabulary adaptation is not entirely new, the use of a helper model (Minerva-3B in this case) specifically trained on the target language to learn a linear mapping is a potentially valuable contribution.  The paper provides a thorough comparison of SAVA with existing methods like FVT and CLP, adding to the novelty.  The analysis of intra-model and inter-model similarities to analyze the impact of different vocabulary adaptation techniques on the embedding structure has an additional element of novelty.

*   **Significance:**  Adaptation of LLMs for languages other than English is an important research area.  The paper addresses a practical problem (high token fertility and slower inference) that hinders the effective use of LLMs in lower-resource languages like Italian. The methods described are potentially transferable to other languages, increasing the impact. The reported reduction in token fertility and parameter size, coupled with competitive performance after adaptation, highlights the practical significance of the approach. Showing the recovery of performance with reduced computational resources directly addresses a key challenge in language model adaptation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly defines the problem of sub-optimal performance of English-centric LLMs in other languages.
    *   **Thorough Comparison:**  A comprehensive comparison of different vocabulary adaptation techniques, including the proposed SAVA method, on multiple downstream tasks and with thorough analyses, is a strong point.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are well-designed and performed, providing strong empirical evidence for the effectiveness of SAVA and the other adaptation methods. The results are clearly presented and analyzed.
    *   **Quantifiable Results:** The paper reports quantifiable metrics (token fertility, parameter counts, downstream task performance) that allow for a clear assessment of the effectiveness of the proposed methods.
    *   **Open Source:** The release of code and models will allow others to build upon and extend the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Translated Benchmarks:**  The evaluation relies heavily on automatically translated benchmarks. Although the authors acknowledge the potential introduction of noise due to this approach, the lack of native Italian benchmarks remains a significant limitation. The paper could have included a manual evaluation of a subset of the translated benchmarks to check the quality of the translation.
    *   **Limited Generative Task Evaluation:** The evaluation could be strengthened by expanding the generative task evaluation, since the results indicated some potential discrepancies for SAVA.
    *   **Helper Model Dependency:** The SAVA method relies on a helper model. While using Minerva-3B is logical in this case, the dependence raises questions about the method's applicability to languages where pre-trained native models are scarce. An ablation study on the helper model is useful but doesn't fully address that concern.
    *   **Transferability:** The methods’ transferability to different architectures or languages is somewhat speculative and needs further evaluation, even though the principles are sound.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research on LLM adaptation for other languages.  The SAVA method and the detailed comparison with existing methods provide a valuable resource for researchers in this area.  The emphasis on reducing token fertility and improving efficiency is also likely to be influential in the development of more practical and sustainable LLMs.  The open-sourcing of code and models will facilitate further research and development.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of LLM adaptation. The introduction of SAVA with the helper model offers a novel strategy for vocabulary adaptation. While the reliance on translated benchmarks and helper model dependency are significant limitations, the thorough experimental evaluation, clear presentation, and open-sourcing of resources enhance its impact.  The emphasis on efficiency (reduced token fertility and parameter counts) addresses a critical need in the field. Considering these factors, the novelty, significance, and weaknesses of the paper, the score is justified.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DyMU: Dynamic Merging and Virtual Unmerging for Efficient VLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17040v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenhailong Wang, Senthil Purushwalkam, Caiming Xiong, Silvio Savarese, Heng Ji, Ran Xu
- **Abstract**: We present DyMU, an efficient, training-free framework that dynamically reduces the computational burden of vision-language models (VLMs) while maintaining high task performance. Our approach comprises two key components. First, Dynamic Token Merging (DToMe) reduces the number of visual token embeddings by merging similar tokens based on image complexity, addressing the inherent inefficiency of fixed-length outputs in vision transformers. Second, Virtual Token Unmerging (VTU) simulates the expected token sequence for large language models (LLMs) by efficiently reconstructing the attention dynamics of a full sequence, thus preserving the downstream performance without additional fine-tuning. Unlike previous approaches, our method dynamically adapts token compression to the content of the image and operates completely training-free, making it readily applicable to most state-of-the-art VLM architectures. Extensive experiments on image and video understanding tasks demonstrate that DyMU can reduce the average visual token count by 32%-85% while achieving comparable performance to full-length models across diverse VLM architectures, including the recently popularized AnyRes-based visual encoders. Furthermore, through qualitative analyses, we demonstrate that DToMe effectively adapts token reduction based on image complexity and, unlike existing systems, provides users more control over computational costs. Project page: https://mikewangwzhl.github.io/dymu/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DYMU (Dynamic Merging and Virtual Unmerging), a novel training-free approach to improve the efficiency of Vision-Language Models (VLMs). DYMU dynamically reduces the number of visual tokens based on image complexity, addressing the inherent inefficiency of fixed-length representations. It comprises two main components: Dynamic Token Merging (DToMe), which reduces the number of visual tokens, and Virtual Token Unmerging (VTU), which simulates the full token sequence for the language model, preserving downstream performance without fine-tuning. The approach is designed to be a plug-and-play solution compatible with various VLM architectures. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of DYMU through experiments on image and video understanding tasks, showing significant reductions in token count while maintaining competitive performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to visual token reduction in VLMs. While token reduction techniques exist, DYMU's key contribution lies in its dynamic, training-free nature and its focus on preserving performance without any additional fine-tuning of the VLM. The integration of DToMe and VTU is also a novel combination, enabling the reduction of computational burden at the vision encoder while preserving downstream VLM performance. The size weighted attention mechanism also contributes to the novelty. The adaptive nature of the token merging based on image complexity is a significant step forward from fixed-length compression approaches.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its ability to make VLMs more computationally efficient without sacrificing performance. This is crucial for deploying VLMs in resource-constrained environments or scaling them to handle high-resolution images and videos.  The training-free aspect of DYMU enhances its practical applicability, as it can be easily integrated into existing VLM pipelines without incurring additional training costs. The results demonstrating substantial token reduction with minimal performance degradation are significant and will likely be valuable to the VLM research community. The qualitative analysis adds further value by showing how DToMe adapts token reduction to image complexity. The method directly tackles the high computational complexity of VLMs due to large visual token sequences and offers a practical solution.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Training-Free:** No additional fine-tuning is needed.
    *   **Dynamic:** Adaptive to image complexity, making it more efficient than fixed-length methods.
    *   **Plug-and-Play:** Works seamlessly with different VLM architectures, vision encoders, and LLMs.
    *   **Significant Token Reduction:** Demonstrates impressive reduction in token count (32%-85%).
    *   **Maintained Performance:** Achieves comparable or better performance compared to full-length models.
    *   **Controllable Cost:** Provides a more fine-grained control over visual token usage and computational cost.
    *   The code for the project is available on Github, enabling other researchers to benefit from the implementation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the limitations of DYMU. Specifically, a deeper discussion is warranted for instances where spatial awareness is required.
    *   While the wall-clock time difference is mentioned as marginal due to PyTorch optimizations, a more detailed benchmarking comparing end-to-end inference times with and without DYMU across different hardware platforms could be helpful to truly demonstrate the speedup gained.
    * The ablations lack clarity by using percentages on one dataset only. The paper would benefit from presenting additional full dataset ablation studies to support the findings.
*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to significantly influence the field of VLMs. The idea of dynamically adjusting visual token lengths based on image complexity is likely to inspire further research in this area. The training-free nature and easy integration of DYMU could lead to its widespread adoption in practical VLM applications. This solution is particularly relevant for resource-constrained applications.
* **Score Justification:**
A score of 8 is warranted for this paper. The core idea is novel and provides a practical solution to a significant challenge in VLMs (computational efficiency). The training-free and plug-and-play nature enhances its adoption potential. The experiments provide solid evidence of its effectiveness across a range of benchmarks and architectures. The weaknesses noted center around depth of analysis and details of optimization, not fundamental flaws in the approach.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Do Words Reflect Beliefs? Evaluating Belief Depth in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17052v1)
- **Authors**: Shariar Kabir, Kevin Esterling, Yue Dong
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly shaping political discourse, yet their responses often display inconsistency when subjected to scrutiny. While prior research has primarily categorized LLM outputs as left- or right-leaning to assess their political stances, a critical question remains: Do these responses reflect genuine internal beliefs or merely surface-level alignment with training data? To address this, we propose a novel framework for evaluating belief depth by analyzing (1) argumentative consistency and (2) uncertainty quantification. We evaluate 12 LLMs on 19 economic policies from the Political Compass Test, challenging their belief stability with both supportive and opposing arguments. Our analysis reveals that LLMs exhibit topic-specific belief stability rather than a uniform ideological stance. Notably, up to 95% of left-leaning models' responses and 89% of right-leaning models' responses remain consistent under the challenge, enabling semantic entropy to achieve high accuracy (AUROC=0.78), effectively distinguishing between surface-level alignment from genuine belief. These findings call into question the assumption that LLMs maintain stable, human-like political ideologies, emphasizing the importance of conducting topic-specific reliability assessments for real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Do Words Reflect Beliefs? Evaluating Belief Depth in Large Language Models" investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) possess genuine political beliefs or merely mimic stances based on training data.  The authors propose a framework to assess "belief depth" by analyzing argumentative consistency and quantifying uncertainty. They evaluate 12 LLMs on 19 economic policies from the Political Compass Test, challenging their stances with both supportive and opposing arguments. The results suggest that LLMs exhibit topic-specific belief stability rather than uniform ideological alignments and that semantic entropy can differentiate surface-level agreement from genuine belief.  The study challenges the assumption that LLMs have stable, human-like political ideologies and emphasizes the need for topic-specific reliability assessments in real-world applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on assessing *belief depth* rather than simply categorizing LLMs as left- or right-leaning.  Prior work has primarily focused on identifying biases and political orientations based on single-prompt responses. This paper goes further by explicitly probing the consistency of these stances under argumentative pressure. The introduction of semantic entropy as a measure to distinguish between surface alignment and genuine belief is also novel. While argumentative consistency has been examined in other contexts, its application within political LLM analysis, combined with uncertainty quantification, is a relatively new approach.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its contribution to a more nuanced understanding of LLMs' political behavior. If LLMs only exhibit superficial alignment, their utility in high-stakes applications like policy analysis and voter engagement is questionable.  The finding that LLMs display topic-specific belief stability is important because it contradicts the simplified view that models maintain a singular coherent ideology.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Research Question:** The paper directly addresses a pertinent and important question about the genuineness of political stances expressed by LLMs.
    * **Well-Defined Framework:** The proposed framework for evaluating belief depth, based on argumentative consistency and uncertainty quantification, provides a concrete methodology for assessing LLM behavior.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experiments using 12 LLMs across a diverse set of economic policies offers substantial empirical evidence.
    * **Rigorous analysis:** The authors considered factors such as topic correlation and fine-tuning effects, resulting in a well-controlled study.
    * **Insightful Findings:** The discovery of topic-specific belief stability is an important contribution to the field.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Political Issues:** While the use of 19 economic policies is reasonable, the generalizability of these findings to other political domains (e.g., social issues, foreign policy) might be limited. The claim that a single factor explains 62% of the variance across the policies can be challenged. A more sophisticated analysis of the latent structure may be required to justify this claim.
    * **Dependence on an NLI Model:** The study relies on an off-the-shelf NLI model to determine agreement or disagreement. The accuracy of the NLI model directly impacts the results, and potential biases or limitations in the NLI model are not fully addressed.
    * **Operationalization of "Genuine Belief":** The concept of "genuine belief" in the context of LLMs is inherently complex.  The paper's definition, based on argumentative consistency, is a reasonable proxy, but it doesn't fully capture the richness of human belief systems. There's a risk of anthropomorphizing LLMs.
    * **Support and counterarguments prompt creation:** The authors use DeepSeek-R1-32B to create support and counterarguments. While this seems like a reasonable method, there is the possibility that these arguments are not strong or may contain the same biases as the statements used in the paper.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper can influence future research by encouraging more critical evaluations of LLMs' political behavior. It highlights the importance of moving beyond simple classification and considering the context-dependent nature of their responses. The proposed framework can be used as a starting point for more sophisticated methods for assessing belief depth and uncertainty in LLMs. It also reinforces the call for developing methods to mitigate biases and improve the reliability of LLMs in politically sensitive applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable and novel contribution by focusing on belief depth in LLMs, which goes beyond simply identifying political biases. The proposed framework is well-defined, and the experiments provide substantial evidence to support the claims. However, the limited scope of political issues, the reliance on the NLI model, and the inherent complexity of defining "genuine belief" in LLMs constrain the impact and scope of the research. The prompt creation for support and counterarguments also creates a limitation. Therefore, taking into account strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7.5/10 reflects the paper's considerable strengths while acknowledging limitations that temper its immediate transformative impact.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Statistical Guarantees in Synthetic Data through Conformal Adversarial Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17058v1)
- **Authors**: Rahul Vishwakarma
- **Abstract**: The generation of high-quality synthetic data presents significant challenges in machine learning research, particularly regarding statistical fidelity and uncertainty quantification. Existing generative models produce compelling synthetic samples but lack rigorous statistical guarantees about their relation to the underlying data distribution, limiting their applicability in critical domains requiring robust error bounds. We address this fundamental limitation by presenting a novel framework that incorporates conformal prediction methodologies into Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs). By integrating multiple conformal prediction paradigms including Inductive Conformal Prediction (ICP), Mondrian Conformal Prediction, Cross-Conformal Prediction, and Venn-Abers Predictors, we establish distribution-free uncertainty quantification in generated samples. This approach, termed Conformalized GAN (cGAN), demonstrates enhanced calibration properties while maintaining the generative power of traditional GANs, producing synthetic data with provable statistical guarantees. We provide rigorous mathematical proofs establishing finite-sample validity guarantees and asymptotic efficiency properties, enabling the reliable application of synthetic data in high-stakes domains including healthcare, finance, and autonomous systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the lack of statistical guarantees in synthetic data generated by Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs). It proposes a novel framework called Conformalized GAN (CGAN) that integrates conformal prediction methodologies (Inductive, Mondrian, Cross, and Venn-Abers) into the GAN architecture. This allows for distribution-free uncertainty quantification in generated samples, providing provable statistical guarantees about their relation to the real data distribution. The CGAN framework modifies the GAN's training objective to include conformal regularization terms. The paper provides theoretical analysis establishing finite-sample validity bounds and empirical validation demonstrating enhanced calibration and downstream utility across multiple datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating conformal prediction into GANs is novel. While both GANs and conformal prediction are established fields, their combination to address the specific problem of statistical guarantees in synthetic data generation is a valuable contribution. The introduction of novel nonconformity measures specifically designed for adversarial training adds further originality.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to make synthetic data generated by GANs more reliable and trustworthy in high-stakes applications like healthcare, finance, and autonomous systems. The statistical guarantees provided by CGANs address a crucial limitation of existing generative models, which lack rigorous error bounds. By providing a way to quantify uncertainty in synthetic data, the paper opens up possibilities for its use in scenarios where confidence and reliability are paramount.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical foundation: The paper provides rigorous mathematical proofs establishing finite-sample validity bounds and asymptotic efficiency properties of CGANs.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation: The empirical evaluation demonstrates the effectiveness of CGANs in enhancing calibration properties while maintaining competitive performance on standard quality metrics. The inclusion of various conformal prediction methods and datasets further strengthens the evaluation.
    *   Practical utility: The paper demonstrates that CGANs achieve improved downstream task accuracy compared to standard GANs, highlighting the practical utility of the framework in real-world applications.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Computational overhead: The integration of conformal prediction introduces additional computational overhead during both training and inference. This could limit the applicability of CGANs in resource-constrained settings.
    *   Limited scope: The paper focuses primarily on static datasets and does not address the challenges of non-stationary data distributions. Extensions to handle dynamic environments would further enhance the practicality of CGANs.
    *   Indirect addressing of GAN issues: The framework does not directly tackle the inherent instabilities in GAN training, such as mode collapse. These remain a factor in the overall performance of the system. Future efforts could focus on adapting the framework to be resilient to such conditions.
*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in generative modeling and synthetic data generation. It highlights the importance of statistical guarantees in high-stakes applications and provides a practical framework for achieving this. Other researchers can build upon this work to develop more efficient and robust conformal prediction methods for GANs and explore their applications in various domains. While the increase in downstream task accuracy is not dramatically higher than standard GANs, the main benefit of this framework is the added certainty that the synthetic data created is valid and correct in it's representation.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant and novel contribution by integrating conformal prediction into GANs to address the crucial need for statistical guarantees in synthetic data. The theoretical analysis, comprehensive evaluation, and demonstration of practical utility are major strengths. The limitations regarding computational overhead, limited scope, and indirect addressing of GAN issues detract from the overall impact. While it's not a perfect solution, it's a significant step forward. Considering these factors, a score of 7.5 reflects the paper's valuable contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Distilling semantically aware orders for autoregressive image generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17069v1)
- **Authors**: Rishav Pramanik, Antoine Poupon, Juan A. Rodriguez, Masih Aminbeidokhti, David Vazquez, Christopher Pal, Zhaozheng Yin, Marco Pedersoli
- **Abstract**: Autoregressive patch-based image generation has recently shown competitive results in terms of image quality and scalability. It can also be easily integrated and scaled within Vision-Language models. Nevertheless, autoregressive models require a defined order for patch generation. While a natural order based on the dictation of the words makes sense for text generation, there is no inherent generation order that exists for image generation. Traditionally, a raster-scan order (from top-left to bottom-right) guides autoregressive image generation models. In this paper, we argue that this order is suboptimal, as it fails to respect the causality of the image content: for instance, when conditioned on a visual description of a sunset, an autoregressive model may generate clouds before the sun, even though the color of clouds should depend on the color of the sun and not the inverse. In this work, we show that first by training a model to generate patches in any-given-order, we can infer both the content and the location (order) of each patch during generation. Secondly, we use these extracted orders to finetune the any-given-order model to produce better-quality images. Through our experiments, we show on two datasets that this new generation method produces better images than the traditional raster-scan approach, with similar training costs and no extra annotations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of patch generation order in autoregressive (AR) image generation models. It argues that traditional raster-scan order is suboptimal because it doesn't respect the inherent causality of image content. The authors propose a method to "distill" semantically aware orders by first training an AR model to generate patches in any order. This allows the model to learn the content and location (order) importance of each patch. Then, these extracted orders are used to fine-tune the model, leading to better image quality compared to raster-scan approaches. The method is validated on Fashion Product and Multimodal CelebA-HQ datasets, demonstrating improved image quality without extra annotations and comparable training costs. They demonstrate, through experiments, that this new method produces images that are qualitatively and quantitatively better.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of learning and adapting patch generation orders in AR image generation is somewhat novel. Previous work has explored arbitrary ordering, but this paper introduces a two-stage approach: first learning the importance of patches and their locations with any-given-order, and then fine-tuning with distilled, semantically-aware orders. The use of relative position encoding and distance regularization adds to the novelty. Also, the demonstration of the learnt orders makes semantically more sense is a novel insight.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact is noteworthy. If the proposed approach consistently outperforms raster-scan methods with similar computational costs, it could become a standard technique in AR image generation. It could lead to better-quality image generation and more controllable AR models. The approach is also aligned with the growing trend of multimodal models, making it potentially useful for tasks beyond image generation. The insights that are extracted from the experiment on background manipulation can provide key insights to guide the future research on AR model.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-described method with good justification for design choices.
    *   Demonstrated improvements over raster-scan and random order generation.
    *   Quantitative results (FID, IS, KID) support claims.
    *   Qualitative results provide visual evidence of better generation orders.
    *   The ablation studies are crucial and provide insight.
    *   Analysis of the learned order is informative.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Computational cost during inference is higher. The author mentions this can be made better using caching tricks. However, it is 2x more expensive even with caching
    *   The improvements, while consistent, aren't overwhelmingly large, at least not on all the metrics.
    *   Limited scale of experiments compared to state-of-the-art generative models.  The models are trained on commodity hardware, which may not fully reveal the potential benefits in larger-scale settings. The evaluation has limited details and the exact setup details
    *   While ablation on distance regularization is provided, the effect is minimal. This aspect could have been refined or removed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's influence will depend on its adoption by the community and the demonstration of its effectiveness on larger-scale datasets and models. The potential for creating more controllable and semantically aware image generation models makes it a significant contribution that can spur further research in the area.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The assigned score is based on the balance between the novel approach and its practical limitations. The problem addressed is relevant, and the solution appears to offer tangible improvements in generation quality, especially given that it does so with little to no added computational cost during training. The experiments could be more comprehensive, and the improvements aren't always drastic. However, the conceptual clarity and solid experimental validation suggest a non-negligible impact on the AR image generation field. The analysis regarding the learnt order also adds another dimension and provides meaningful insights for the future research

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Robo-Troj: Attacking LLM-based Task Planners
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17070v1)
- **Authors**: Mohaiminul Al Nahian, Zainab Altaweel, David Reitano, Sabbir Ahmed, Saumitra Lohokare, Shiqi Zhang, Adnan Siraj Rakin
- **Abstract**: Robots need task planning methods to achieve goals that require more than individual actions. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance in task planning. LLMs can generate a step-by-step solution using a description of actions and the goal. Despite the successes in LLM-based task planning, there is limited research studying the security aspects of those systems. In this paper, we develop Robo-Troj, the first multi-trigger backdoor attack for LLM-based task planners, which is the main contribution of this work. As a multi-trigger attack, Robo-Troj is trained to accommodate the diversity of robot application domains. For instance, one can use unique trigger words, e.g., "herical", to activate a specific malicious behavior, e.g., cutting hand on a kitchen robot. In addition, we develop an optimization method for selecting the trigger words that are most effective. Through demonstrating the vulnerability of LLM-based planners, we aim to promote the development of secured robot systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Robo-Troj: Attacking LLM-based Task Planners":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Robo-Troj," the first backdoor attack specifically designed for Large Language Model (LLM)-based robot task planners.  It focuses on compromising LLM systems that generate step-by-step plans for robots to achieve goals. The attack involves poisoning the soft-prompt tuning process of the LLM by injecting specific trigger words.  When these triggers are present in a user's task description, the LLM generates malicious task plans (e.g., a kitchen robot instructed to cut someone's hand). The paper presents a multi-trigger backdoor optimization (MBO) approach to generate effective trigger words, allowing for diverse malicious behaviors.  The authors demonstrate the attack's effectiveness in simulation and on a real robot platform, highlighting the vulnerability of these systems and advocating for the development of more secure robot intelligence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty of the paper lies in addressing the *security* aspects of LLM-based task planners. While there has been considerable research into using LLMs for robot task planning, the paper correctly points out the gap in understanding and mitigating security vulnerabilities.  The "Robo-Troj" attack and the MBO trigger generation strategy are novel contributions within this specific intersection of robotics and LLM security. The idea of multi-trigger attacks further increases the paper's novelty.

**Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the potential real-world risks associated with compromised robot systems. As robots become increasingly integrated into our lives (homes, healthcare, industry), the ability to manipulate their behavior through backdoor attacks poses a significant threat. By demonstrating the feasibility of such attacks, the paper raises important concerns about the security and safety of LLM-driven robotics.

**Strengths:**

*   **First of Its Kind:** It is, according to the authors' claims, the first study of backdoor attacks specifically targeting LLM-based robotic task planners.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the threat model and outlines the attacker's capabilities and limitations.
*   **Multi-Trigger Backdoor Optimization:** The development of MBO is a valuable contribution, allowing for more diverse and potentially stealthier attacks.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The evaluation of Robo-Troj in both simulation (VirtualHome) and on a real robot provides compelling evidence of the attack's effectiveness. The use of standard evaluation metrics (ASR, CDA, BLEU) strengthens the evaluation.
*  **Ablation Studies:** The Ablation Study to generate Triggers for different malicious behaviors improves the paper's credibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of LLMs:** The paper focuses on relatively smaller LLMs (GPT-J-6B, GPT2-Large, Llama-2-7B).  While justifiable due to accessibility, it does leave open the question of how well the attack scales to more powerful (and often proprietary) LLMs like GPT-4 or Gemini. The paper mentions the inaccessibility of the newer LLMs as the reason. The results might vary significantly on models with more inherent safety measures.
*   **Simplified Environments:** The simulated environment (VirtualHome) and the real-world robot setup are relatively controlled. The complexity of real-world scenarios could introduce new challenges for the attacker.
*   **Defense Discussion is limited:** The discussion on potential defense mechanisms is somewhat brief. While acknowledging that existing defenses might not be directly applicable, a more in-depth exploration of possible mitigation strategies would strengthen the paper.
*   **Generalizability Concerns**: While the authors attempted to address the generalizability concerns through various datasets, the lack of datasets in industrial or healthcare limits the robustness of the study.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

*   It can spark further investigation into the security vulnerabilities of LLM-based robotic systems.
*   It can motivate the development of new defense mechanisms specifically designed to counter backdoor attacks in these systems.
*   It can raise awareness among researchers and practitioners about the importance of security considerations when deploying LLMs in safety-critical applications.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering both the novelty and significance of the work, along with its strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of 7. This is not a groundbreaking contribution, but the Robo-Troj attack exposes a concerning vulnerability within an increasingly important field. The experimental evidence is strong, and the MBO trigger generation strategy is a valuable technical contribution. The weaknesses lie primarily in the limitations of the experimental setup and the relative immaturity of the field of LLM security for robotics. The paper raises an important flag and provides a solid foundation for future research.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Physics-guided and fabrication-aware inverse design of photonic devices using diffusion models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17077v1)
- **Authors**: Dongjin Seo, Soobin Um, Sangbin Lee, Jong Chul Ye, Haejun Chung
- **Abstract**: Designing free-form photonic devices is fundamentally challenging due to the vast number of possible geometries and the complex requirements of fabrication constraints. Traditional inverse-design approaches--whether driven by human intuition, global optimization, or adjoint-based gradient methods--often involve intricate binarization and filtering steps, while recent deep learning strategies demand prohibitively large numbers of simulations (10^5 to 10^6). To overcome these limitations, we present AdjointDiffusion, a physics-guided framework that integrates adjoint sensitivity gradients into the sampling process of diffusion models. AdjointDiffusion begins by training a diffusion network on a synthetic, fabrication-aware dataset of binary masks. During inference, we compute the adjoint gradient of a candidate structure and inject this physics-based guidance at each denoising step, steering the generative process toward high figure-of-merit (FoM) solutions without additional post-processing. We demonstrate our method on two canonical photonic design problems--a bent waveguide and a CMOS image sensor color router--and show that our method consistently outperforms state-of-the-art nonlinear optimizers (such as MMA and SLSQP) in both efficiency and manufacturability, while using orders of magnitude fewer simulations (approximately 2 x 10^2) than pure deep learning approaches (approximately 10^5 to 10^6). By eliminating complex binarization schedules and minimizing simulation overhead, AdjointDiffusion offers a streamlined, simulation-efficient, and fabrication-aware pipeline for next-generation photonic device design. Our open-source implementation is available at https://github.com/dongjin-seo2020/AdjointDiffusion.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents *AdjointDiffusion*, a novel framework for inverse design of photonic devices. It combines adjoint sensitivity analysis with denoising diffusion probabilistic models. The approach involves training a diffusion model on a synthetic, fabrication-aware dataset of binary masks. During inference, the adjoint gradient of a candidate structure is computed and injected into each denoising step to guide the generative process toward high-performance solutions. The method is demonstrated on two canonical photonic design problems: a bent waveguide and a CMOS image sensor color router. The authors claim that *AdjointDiffusion* outperforms traditional nonlinear optimizers and deep-learning approaches in terms of efficiency, manufacturability, and reduced simulation requirements. A key advantage highlighted is the elimination of complex binarization steps, streamlining the design process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of adjoint sensitivity analysis within the denoising process of diffusion models for inverse design. While diffusion models and adjoint methods are individually established, their synergistic combination in this context represents a meaningful advancement. The use of a *data-free* approach, minimizing reliance on large simulation datasets during training, also contributes to the novelty. Specifically, existing work either employed deep learning in a more data-intensive fashion or did not embed adjoint gradients into the sampling loop of diffusion models. This is a clear contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in photonic device design: the computational cost and complexity associated with traditional inverse design methods. *AdjointDiffusion's* claimed reduction in simulation requirements (2x10^2 versus 10^5-10^6 for pure deep learning) is potentially significant, making inverse design more accessible and efficient. The focus on fabrication-aware design is also valuable, addressing a practical consideration often overlooked. The results showcasing performance improvements over state-of-the-art nonlinear optimizers in both efficiency and manufacturability strengthen the significance of the work. The streamlining of the design pipeline via elimination of complex binarization adds to the method's practical value.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing inverse design methods.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The integration of adjoint sensitivity analysis and diffusion models is innovative and well-motivated.
    *   **Performance Demonstrations:** The results on the bent waveguide and CMOS image sensor color router problems provide compelling evidence of the method's effectiveness.
    *   **Data-Free Nature:** The approach minimizes reliance on computationally expensive simulations during the training phase, a key advantage.
    *   **Fabrication-Aware Design:** The method addresses practical fabrication constraints, improving real-world applicability.
    *   **Open Source:** The availability of the implementation fosters reproducibility and further development.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Problem Domain:** The evaluation is limited to two specific photonic design problems. Expanding the evaluation to a wider range of devices and design objectives would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** The improvement over state-of-the-art might be considered incremental rather than a revolutionary breakthrough.
    *   **Post-Processing Step:** While the proposed approach reduces the need for explicit binarization during sampling, there remains a simple post-processing step.
    *   **Reliance on existing tooling**: The paper depends significantly on external simulation software.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the claims hold up under further scrutiny and broader application, *AdjointDiffusion* could have a significant impact on the field of photonic device design, potentially enabling faster and more efficient development of high-performance devices. The method's reduced simulation requirements could democratize inverse design, making it accessible to researchers with limited computational resources.

**Justification of Score:**

While *AdjointDiffusion* presents a valuable contribution to the field by successfully integrating adjoint sensitivity analysis and denoising diffusion models, some limitations prevent it from receiving a higher score. The method's improvement over existing techniques, while demonstrated, is somewhat incremental. The evaluation is limited to two canonical problems, so it is uncertain whether these will be effective in different designs. Overall, the contribution is significant for its novelty, practical benefits, and potential for future development.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: physics.optics
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging LLMs as Meta-Judges: A Multi-Agent Framework for Evaluating LLM Judgments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17087v1)
- **Authors**: Yuran Li, Jama Hussein Mohamud, Chongren Sun, Di Wu, Benoit Boulet
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are being widely applied across various fields, but as tasks become more complex, evaluating their responses is increasingly challenging. Compared to human evaluators, the use of LLMs to support performance evaluation offers a more efficient alternative. However, most studies focus mainly on aligning LLMs' judgments with human preferences, overlooking the existence of biases and mistakes in human judgment. Furthermore, how to select suitable LLM judgments given multiple potential LLM responses remains underexplored. To address these two aforementioned issues, we propose a three-stage meta-judge selection pipeline: 1) developing a comprehensive rubric with GPT-4 and human experts, 2) using three advanced LLM agents to score judgments, and 3) applying a threshold to filter out low-scoring judgments. Compared to methods using a single LLM as both judge and meta-judge, our pipeline introduces multi-agent collaboration and a more comprehensive rubric. Experimental results on the JudgeBench dataset show about 15.55\% improvement compared to raw judgments and about 8.37\% improvement over the single-agent baseline. Our work demonstrates the potential of LLMs as meta-judges and lays the foundation for future research on constructing preference datasets for LLM-as-a-judge reinforcement learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Leveraging LLMs as Meta-Judges: A Multi-Agent Framework for Evaluating LLM Judgments":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a three-stage framework for improving the quality of Large Language Model (LLM) judgments by using other LLMs as "meta-judges." The framework consists of: 1) creating a detailed rubric using GPT-4 and human experts; 2) employing multiple LLM agents to score the judgments based on the rubric, using strategies like weighted averaging, majority voting, and panel discussion; and 3) applying a threshold to filter out low-scoring judgments. The aim is to enhance the precision of LLM-based evaluations, reducing reliance on human annotation and addressing potential biases in both human and LLM judgments. Experiments on the JudgeBench dataset demonstrate improvements in precision compared to raw judgments and a single-agent baseline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in its systematic approach to meta-judging LLM judgments, combining a detailed rubric design, a multi-agent collaboration strategy, and a threshold-based selection mechanism.  While the idea of using LLMs to evaluate other LLMs is not entirely new, this paper goes further than previous work by using a more complex and nuanced methodology. Specifically, the explicit design of a rubric using both human expertise and GPT-4 is somewhat novel. The comparison of different multi-agent strategies (weighted averaging, majority voting, panel discussion) in the context of meta-judging provides useful empirical insights. However, the panel discussion strategy shows only moderate success.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem: ensuring the quality and reliability of LLM-based evaluations.  As LLMs are increasingly used to evaluate other LLMs and generate feedback for reinforcement learning, a robust and automated evaluation pipeline becomes essential. Improving the reliability of such judgments could have significant implications for the development of more capable and trustworthy LLMs. Furthermore, the paper's methodology could be valuable for creating preference datasets for LLM-as-a-judge reinforcement learning, ultimately leading to better evaluation capabilities. The experimental results, while using a specific dataset, provide empirical evidence to support the feasibility of the proposed framework.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** The three-stage framework provides a well-defined and structured methodology for meta-judging.
    *   **Multi-agent collaboration:** Exploring different multi-agent strategies is a valuable contribution, offering insights into the strengths and weaknesses of each approach.
