### Automatically Generating Rules of Malicious Software Packages via Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17198v1)
- **Authors**: XiangRui Zhang, HaoYu Chen, Yongzhong He, Wenjia Niu, Qiang Li
- **Abstract**: Today's security tools predominantly rely on predefined rules crafted by experts, making them poorly adapted to the emergence of software supply chain attacks. To tackle this limitation, we propose a novel tool, RuleLLM, which leverages large language models (LLMs) to automate rule generation for OSS ecosystems. RuleLLM extracts metadata and code snippets from malware as its input, producing YARA and Semgrep rules that can be directly deployed in software development. Specifically, the rule generation task involves three subtasks: crafting rules, refining rules, and aligning rules. To validate RuleLLM's effectiveness, we implemented a prototype system and conducted experiments on the dataset of 1,633 malicious packages. The results are promising that RuleLLM generated 763 rules (452 YARA and 311 Semgrep) with a precision of 85.2\% and a recall of 91.8\%, outperforming state-of-the-art (SOTA) tools and scored-based approaches. We further analyzed generated rules and proposed a rule taxonomy: 11 categories and 38 subcategories.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RULELLM, a novel tool that automates the generation of YARA and Semgrep rules for detecting malicious software packages in open-source ecosystems using large language models (LLMs). RULELLM extracts metadata and code snippets from malware, then crafts, refines, and aligns rules, addressing challenges like code redundancy, LLM input limitations, and hallucination. The authors evaluated RULELLM on a dataset of 1,633 malicious packages, achieving a precision of 85.2% and a recall of 91.8%, outperforming existing tools and scored-based approaches. They also propose a rule taxonomy with 11 categories and 38 subcategories based on an analysis of the generated rules.  The tool and generated rules have been released to the research community.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The use of LLMs to *automate* rule generation for detecting malicious software in OSS ecosystems is a good idea. Traditionally, security rules are crafted manually, which is a time-consuming and expertise-demanding process. RULELLM attempts to address this limitation by automating the rule generation workflow. The decomposition of the rule generation into crafting, refining, and aligning steps, while not entirely novel on its own, provides a structured approach for LLM-based security rule creation and aids in mitigating the hallucination problems of LLMs.
*   **Significance:** The ability to automatically generate security rules could have a significant impact on the security of open-source software. By creating rules that are compatible with existing security tools such as YARA and Semgrep, RULELLM has the potential to enhance the detection capabilities of those tools and reduce the dependence on human security experts. The high precision and recall reported in the experimental results suggest that RULELLM can effectively identify malicious packages. The taxonomy of rules provides valuable insights into the characteristics and applications of the generated rules, potentially facilitating future research and development in this area.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Automation:** Automating the rule creation process addresses a key bottleneck in software security.
    *   **Structured Approach:** The multi-stage (crafting, refining, aligning) approach is well-designed to handle LLM limitations.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results on a real-world dataset of malicious packages demonstrate the effectiveness of RULELLM.
    *   **Open Source:** Releasing the tool and generated rules promotes reproducibility and further research in this area.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependency:** The performance of RULELLM heavily relies on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. The potential for data leakage given the models have already been trained on code is high.
    *   **Hallucinations and Errors:** While the paper addresses the issue of LLM hallucinations, it acknowledges that they are still inevitable. The rule alignment step is an attempt to address this, but its effectiveness may vary depending on the specific error. The limitations in the current implementation of the LLM to deal with obfuscated code are also worth noting.
    *   **Coverage of Edge Cases:**  The evaluation doesn't deeply probe the tool's performance on highly complex or evasive malware. While the results are positive, a more detailed exploration of failure cases is necessary.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While promising, the tool primarily addresses rule generation. It does not tackle related problems like alert fatigue or rule maintenance, which are crucial for practical deployment.
    *   **Generalizability:** The results are based on PyPI packages. The performance might vary in different ecosystems like NPM or RubyGems, as the structure and metadata formats differ.

*   **Impact:** RULELLM has the potential to impact the software security landscape by empowering developers and security analysts to more effectively detect malicious packages in open-source ecosystems. It can complement existing security tools by providing a means to create detection rules for both known and emerging threats.
*   **Justification for Score:**  While the general idea of using LLMs for rule generation is not entirely new, RULELLM makes a contribution through its structured approach, empirical validation, and release of the tool and generated rules.  The results are promising, showing significant improvements over existing methods. However, the strong dependency on LLM performance and the open questions on handling edge cases hold back a higher score. The problem of data leakage has not been sufficiently addressed. RULELLM presents a good step in automating security rule creation, but it is not yet a complete solution to securing OSS ecosystems. A score of 7 reflects its novelty, significance, and solid evaluation, while acknowledging its dependence on LLMs and limitations in dealing with complex threats and other stages of threat management.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### A RAG-Based Multi-Agent LLM System for Natural Hazard Resilience and Adaptation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17200v1)
- **Authors**: Yangxinyu Xie, Bowen Jiang, Tanwi Mallick, Joshua David Bergerson, John K. Hutchison, Duane R. Verner, Jordan Branham, M. Ross Alexander, Robert B. Ross, Yan Feng, Leslie-Anne Levy, Weijie Su, Camillo J. Taylor
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are a transformational capability at the frontier of artificial intelligence and machine learning that can support decision-makers in addressing pressing societal challenges such as extreme natural hazard events. As generalized models, LLMs often struggle to provide context-specific information, particularly in areas requiring specialized knowledge. In this work we propose a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG)-based multi-agent LLM system to support analysis and decision-making in the context of natural hazards and extreme weather events. As a proof of concept, we present WildfireGPT, a specialized system focused on wildfire hazards. The architecture employs a user-centered, multi-agent design to deliver tailored risk insights across diverse stakeholder groups. By integrating natural hazard and extreme weather projection data, observational datasets, and scientific literature through an RAG framework, the system ensures both the accuracy and contextual relevance of the information it provides. Evaluation across ten expert-led case studies demonstrates that WildfireGPT significantly outperforms existing LLM-based solutions for decision support.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WildfireGPT, a novel retrieval-augmented generation (RAG)-based multi-agent LLM system designed to enhance natural hazard resilience and adaptation, specifically focusing on wildfire hazards. The system aims to support decision-makers by providing context-specific and actionable information synthesized from diverse sources such as natural hazard projections, observational datasets, and scientific literature. The architecture employs a user-centered, multi-agent design to tailor risk insights across diverse stakeholder groups. The paper details the system's design, including its user profile agent, planning agent, and analyst agent, along with interactive visualizations of geospatial data. The effectiveness of WildfireGPT is evaluated through ten expert-led case studies. The evaluation includes a comparison against existing LLM-based solutions (ChatClimate and Perplexity AI), a qualitative ablation study, and a detailed assessment of case study responses through fidelity verification and expert evaluation. The paper also explores the potential of using LLM-as-a-judge for scalable, automated evaluation. The results indicate that WildfireGPT outperforms existing solutions in providing relevant and actionable information for wildfire decision support.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a system integrating multiple agents and retrieval-augmented generation for natural hazard management. While LLMs and RAG are not novel in themselves, their specific application and architecture within a multi-agent framework for natural hazard resilience, particularly wildfire management, demonstrates a degree of novelty. The focus on creating a personalized and context-aware experience through the user profile and planning agents adds a practical dimension rarely seen in general LLM applications. The multi-stage evaluation methodology, integrating expert assessment with exploring LLM-as-a-judge, also represents a novel approach in this domain. However, some components of the architecture and evaluation utilize existing techniques, so it is difficult to call this a revolutionary advance.

**Significance:**

The work addresses a significant challenge: translating scientific knowledge into actionable risk reduction strategies for diverse stakeholders in the context of natural hazards. The ability of WildfireGPT to synthesize information from various sources and provide tailored recommendations has the potential to improve decision-making processes in wildfire management. The findings highlight the limitations of existing LLM-based solutions in this domain and offer a more practical and effective alternative. This work is helpful for policymakers, land managers, and infrastructure operators working to reduce wildfire risks. Furthermore, the comprehensive evaluation methodology and the exploration of LLM-as-a-judge for quality assurance provide valuable insights for future research in this area.

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Application:** Focuses on a real-world problem with practical implications for decision-makers.
*   **Comprehensive Data Integration:** Combines diverse data sources (projections, observations, socioeconomics, literature).
*   **User-Centered Design:** Employs a multi-agent system to personalize responses to diverse stakeholders.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** Includes comparative analysis, ablation studies, expert evaluations, and exploration of LLM-as-a-judge.
*   **Actionable Recommendations:** Provides contextually appropriate and implementable recommendations for infrastructure resilience.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:** The system is specifically designed for wildfire management, so its applicability to other natural hazards may be limited. However, the conceptual framework could be adapted.
*   **Dependency on Data Quality:** The accuracy and reliability of WildfireGPT's outputs depend on the quality and completeness of the underlying data sources.
*   **Computational Cost:** Multi-agent systems using GPT-4 and similar models can be computationally expensive, limiting their scalability and real-time deployment potential. The paper doesn't clearly address the computational cost of the approach.
*   **Typos & Minor Errors:** The paper contains some typos and minor hallucination issues with GPT-4, such as minor inaccuracies and occasional failures in transitioning between steps. This shows the immaturity of the model.
*   **Potential for Bias:** The selection of data sources and the design of the LLM prompts could introduce biases, potentially affecting the fairness and equity of the recommendations. This could be expanded in the paper.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the evaluation, the paper provides a novel practical solution for natural hazards, the proposed architecture and the implemented evaluation methodology being the major strengths of the paper. This paper is however limited by some aspects that would improve the overall score, such as computational limitations, reliability due to GPT-4 errors, and the dependence on high-quality data. While there are a few limitations to the paper, the integration of diverse datasets into a multi-agent system using RAG makes the paper more practical, which has strong implications for the future direction of AI-assisted disaster mitigation strategies.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### High-Fidelity And Complex Test Data Generation For Real-World SQL Code Generation Services
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17203v1)
- **Authors**: Shivasankari Kannan, Yeounoh Chung, Amita Gondi, Tristan Swadell, Fatma Ozcan
- **Abstract**: The demand for high-fidelity test data is paramount in industrial settings where access to production data is largely restricted. Traditional data generation methods often fall short, struggling with low-fidelity and the ability to model complex data structures and semantic relationships that are critical for testing complex SQL code generation services like Natural Language to SQL (NL2SQL). In this paper, we address the critical need for generating syntactically correct and semantically ``meaningful'' mock data for complex schema that includes columns with nested structures that we frequently encounter in Google SQL code generation workloads. We highlight the limitations of existing approaches used in production, particularly their inability to handle large and complex schema, as well as the lack of semantically coherent test data that lead to limited test coverage. We demonstrate that by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) and incorporating strategic pre- and post-processing steps, we can generate realistic high-fidelity test data that adheres to complex structural constraints and maintains semantic integrity to the test targets (SQL queries/functions). This approach supports comprehensive testing of complex SQL queries involving joins, aggregations, and even deeply nested subqueries, ensuring robust evaluation of SQL code generation services, like NL2SQL and SQL Code Assistant services. Our results demonstrate the practical utility of an out-of-the-box LLM (\textit{gemini}) based test data generation for industrial SQL code generation services where generating realistic test data is essential due to the frequent unavailability of production datasets.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the challenge of generating high-fidelity test data for real-world SQL code generation services, specifically within industrial settings where access to production data is restricted due to privacy concerns.  The authors propose a novel approach that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically Gemini, combined with pre- and post-processing steps to generate realistic mock data that adheres to complex schema structures, including nested data types common in GoogleSQL. The paper demonstrates that this method improves test coverage for complex SQL queries (joins, aggregations, subqueries) compared to traditional methods like manual creation or production data sampling. The generated test data is shown to be semantically coherent and capable of uncovering edge cases.  The authors evaluate their approach using two Google services: SQL Code Assistant and NL2SQL. The results show significant improvements in test coverage and data quality.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its practical application of LLMs to a specific industrial problem: generating realistic and semantically coherent test data for complex SQL queries when direct access to production data is limited. While LLMs have been used for synthetic data generation before, the focus on complex nested schemas (prototypes) and semantic coherence in the *context of SQL testing* represents a valuable contribution. The authors don't introduce a completely new LLM architecture or training technique, but rather a well-engineered *pipeline* for applying existing LLM capabilities effectively.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical impact of improved test data generation on SQL code quality and reliability.  In large-scale, production environments, ensuring the correctness of SQL code is critical. The paper provides a solution to overcome the limitations of current methods which either struggle to handle the complex structure of the data or they require direct access to the data, potentially violating user privacy. It enables broader testing, including edge cases. This can directly translate to improved code quality, reduced bugs, and increased trustworthiness in SQL code generation services. The reliance on Gemini, while presenting a strong application, is simultaneously a limiting factor.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Real-world relevance:** The paper tackles a genuinely important problem in a realistic industrial setting (Google).
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The limitations of existing methods are clearly articulated.
    *   **Well-defined solution:** The architecture of the LLM-based test data generation pipeline is well-explained.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in test coverage and data quality.
    *   **Emphasis on semantic coherence:**  Recognizing and addressing the need for data to be semantically relevant to queries is a significant strength.
    *   **Addresses complex datatypes:** The paper effectively addresses the complex nested data-types (including protos). The ability to generate test data for these is crucial for the target application.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on Gemini:** The reliance on a specific, proprietary LLM (Gemini) makes the solution less generally applicable and harder to reproduce outside Google. While Gemini is a powerful model, results could vary with other LLMs. A more generalized approach would enhance the paper's long-term value.
    *   **Limited generalization beyond GoogleSQL:** The paper is specifically tailored to GoogleSQL. While the core ideas are potentially transferable to other SQL dialects, the practical implementation details might need adaptation.
    *   **Limited evaluation of the NL2SQL result:** While the paper focuses on improving SQL query test coverage, it doesn't fully demonstrate or quantify how the improved test data directly improves the accuracy of NL2SQL systems. A study linking the improvements in test coverage with improvements in NL2SQL prediction quality would significantly strengthen the paper.
    *   **Missing details on Hallucinations mitigations** The paper mentions using the LLM-as-a-judge strategy to reduce hallucinations. However, details of the validation rules and how the extracted SQL constrains are used would have been a useful addition to the paper.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence how SQL code generation services are developed and tested, particularly in environments with strict data privacy constraints. It demonstrates a viable path for leveraging LLMs for this purpose. The approach could inspire similar work in other domains where realistic synthetic data is needed for testing and validation. The influence is, however, dependent on LLMs becoming more accessible and easier to use.

**Justification of Score**

I am assigning a score of **7**. The paper demonstrates a practical and valuable solution to a real-world problem, particularly useful in industrial settings. The experimental results are solid, and the emphasis on semantic coherence is commendable. However, the strong dependency on Gemini, the limited evaluation of NL2SQL accuracy improvements, and the specific focus on GoogleSQL somewhat limit the generality and long-term impact of the work. It is a well-executed case study with potential for broader adoption with further generalization. The methods for hallucinations mitigation also needs to be detailed.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 7/10

### Visual and textual prompts for enhancing emotion recognition in video
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17224v1)
- **Authors**: Zhifeng Wang, Qixuan Zhang, Peter Zhang, Wenjia Niu, Kaihao Zhang, Ramesh Sankaranarayana, Sabrina Caldwell, Tom Gedeon
- **Abstract**: Vision Large Language Models (VLLMs) exhibit promising potential for multi-modal understanding, yet their application to video-based emotion recognition remains limited by insufficient spatial and contextual awareness. Traditional approaches, which prioritize isolated facial features, often neglect critical non-verbal cues such as body language, environmental context, and social interactions, leading to reduced robustness in real-world scenarios. To address this gap, we propose Set-of-Vision-Text Prompting (SoVTP), a novel framework that enhances zero-shot emotion recognition by integrating spatial annotations (e.g., bounding boxes, facial landmarks), physiological signals (facial action units), and contextual cues (body posture, scene dynamics, others' emotions) into a unified prompting strategy. SoVTP preserves holistic scene information while enabling fine-grained analysis of facial muscle movements and interpersonal dynamics. Extensive experiments show that SoVTP achieves substantial improvements over existing visual prompting methods, demonstrating its effectiveness in enhancing VLLMs' video emotion recognition capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Set-of-Vision-Text Prompting (SoVTP), a novel framework designed to enhance zero-shot emotion recognition in videos using Vision Large Language Models (VLLMs). SoVTP works by incorporating spatial annotations (bounding boxes, facial landmarks), physiological signals (facial action units), and contextual cues (body posture, scene dynamics, others' emotions) into a unified prompting strategy. The authors argue that traditional approaches focusing solely on facial features often miss crucial non-verbal cues, leading to reduced robustness in real-world scenarios. SoVTP aims to preserve holistic scene information while allowing for fine-grained analysis.  Experimental results demonstrate SoVTP's effectiveness in improving VLLMs' emotion recognition capabilities compared to existing visual prompting techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of SoVTP – enriching prompts with multiple layers of visual and textual information – is reasonably novel in the context of *zero-shot* emotion recognition. Prior work has explored visual prompting, but the specific combination of spatial information, physiological cues, contextual information, and emotion of others within a scene, all integrated into a single prompting strategy, represents a distinct contribution.  The incremental or staged approach is also noteworthy, allowing for an understanding of how different elements contribute to the final result.

*   **Significance:** Emotion recognition is a challenging task, and improving the accuracy and robustness of VLLMs in this domain has practical applications in various fields (HCI, VR, mental health, etc.). SoVTP addresses a known weakness of existing VLLMs by incorporating contextual awareness, which is often crucial for interpreting emotions in real-world scenarios. The results demonstrate a significant improvement over baseline methods, suggesting a real advancement.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Holistic Approach:** SoVTP's strength lies in its holistic integration of multiple data sources to create a more context-aware model for emotion recognition.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of SoVTP compared to various existing visual prompting methods. The detailed comparisons across different difficulty levels and emotion categories provide compelling evidence.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study is well-designed and provides valuable insights into the contributions of individual components of the SoVTP framework. It demonstrates that each modality (action units, context, body language, emotions of others) adds value to the final outcome.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The qualitative analysis highlights how the SoVTP approach is able to retain a broader field of view, capturing body language and contextual information.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Complexity:** The paper doesn't explicitly address the computational overhead associated with SoVTP.  Extracting all the information used (facial landmarks, action units, scene context, others' emotions) likely adds significant complexity compared to simpler prompting methods.
    *   **Dependence on Existing Tools:** SoVTP relies on existing tools for face detection, landmark extraction, and action unit analysis. The accuracy of SoVTP is therefore limited by the performance of these underlying tools.  If these pre-processing steps introduce errors, it could negatively impact the overall performance of SoVTP.
    *   **Limited Dataset:** While the authors make efforts to ensure dataset quality through multiple annotation steps, the reliance on a single news source (ABC News) might limit the generalizability of the results. A more diverse dataset encompassing different cultural contexts and scenarios would strengthen the findings.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in emotion recognition with VLLMs. It encourages researchers to move beyond simple facial feature analysis and to consider the broader context in which emotions are expressed. SoVTP provides a concrete framework for achieving this goal. Furthermore, the release of the code and benchmark dataset will facilitate further research in this area.

**Justification of Score:**

While SoVTP is a promising approach with demonstrably improved performance, it's not without its limitations. The computational complexity and reliance on existing tools need to be addressed in future work. The lack of broader dataset also means further evaluations are necessary. Overall, the paper provides a well-executed proof-of-concept, establishing a promising direction.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### FLAG: Formal and LLM-assisted SVA Generation for Formal Specifications of On-Chip Communication Protocols
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17226v1)
- **Authors**: Yu-An Shih, Annie Lin, Aarti Gupta, Sharad Malik
- **Abstract**: Formal specifications of on-chip communication protocols are crucial for system-on-chip (SoC) design and verification. However, manually constructing these formal specifications from informal documents remains a tedious and error-prone task. Although recent efforts have used Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate SystemVerilog Assertion (SVA) properties from design documents for Register-Transfer Level (RTL) design verification, in our experience these approaches have not shown promise in generating SVA properties for communication protocols. Since protocol specification documents are unstructured and ambiguous in nature, LLMs often fail to extract the necessary information and end up generating irrelevant or even incorrect properties. We propose FLAG, a two-stage framework to help construct formal protocol specifications from informal documents. In the first stage, a predefined template set is used to generate candidate SVA properties. To avoid missing necessary properties, we develop a grammar-based approach to generate comprehensive template sets that capture critical signal behaviors for various communication protocols. In the second stage, we utilize unambiguous timing diagrams in conjunction with textual descriptions from the specification documents to filter out incorrect properties. A formal approach is first implemented to check the candidate properties and filter out those inconsistent with the timing diagrams. An LLM is then consulted to further remove incorrect properties with respect to the textual description, obtaining the final property set. Experiments on various open-source communication protocols demonstrate the effectiveness of FLAG in generating SVA properties from informal documents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FLAG, a two-stage framework designed to automate the generation of formal specifications (SystemVerilog Assertions - SVAs) for on-chip communication protocols from informal documentation.  FLAG addresses the limitations of directly using Large Language Models (LLMs) for this task, which often struggle with unstructured protocol specifications. The first stage uses a grammar-based approach to generate candidate SVA properties from predefined templates. The second stage filters these candidates by (1) formally checking consistency with timing diagrams using SAT solvers and (2) leveraging LLMs to further refine the properties based on textual descriptions from the documentation. Experiments on various open-source communication protocols demonstrate FLAG's effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**

    *   The paper's novelty lies in its **integration of multiple techniques** to address a specific problem in hardware verification.  While template-based assertion generation and LLM-based methods exist, the combination of grammar-driven generation, formal verification using SAT, and LLM-based filtering represents a distinct approach.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The components themselves are not radically new. Template-based generation is a well-established technique, and LLMs have been applied to assertion generation before. SAT solving for verification is also mature. The key contribution is the *orchestration* of these elements to address the inherent difficulties of complex protocol specifications.
    *   **Limited by Focus:** The focus is narrowly on on-chip communication protocols, limiting the generalizability of the work.  The specific grammar and templates may not be easily adapted to other types of hardware specifications.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Practical Problem:** Formal specification of communication protocols is a time-consuming and error-prone task.  Automating this process can significantly improve design and verification productivity.
    *   **Improved Accuracy:**  The combination of formal and LLM-based filtering offers the potential for higher accuracy than relying solely on LLMs. LLMs are notoriously prone to generating irrelevant or incorrect properties due to the ambiguous nature of natural language specifications.
    *   **Potential for Adoption:** The framework could be integrated into existing EDA tool flows.  However, the reliance on specific grammars and templates may hinder widespread adoption unless a comprehensive and easily extensible library of these resources is developed.
    *   **LLM Dependence Risk:** The framework's performance is tied to the capabilities of the LLM used. Future improvements in LLMs could enhance the framework's accuracy, but conversely, limitations of the chosen LLM also restrict its potential.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** While the paper reports results on several protocols, the scalability of the grammar-based approach to *extremely* complex protocols remains a question.  The number of candidate properties generated could become unmanageable, even with the SAT-based filtering.
    *   **Manual Effort:** The user is still required to manually extract timing diagrams and textual descriptions. Fully automating this extraction process remains a challenge.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a specific problem in hardware verification.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** FLAG provides a structured and well-defined methodology.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:**  The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the framework on a range of communication protocols.
    *   **Analysis of LLM limitations:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the LLM's strengths and weaknesses.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The approach is tailored to on-chip communication protocols, limiting its broader applicability.
    *   **Manual Effort:**  Manual extraction of timing diagrams and textual descriptions remains a bottleneck.
    *   **Scalability Issues:** Potential scalability issues with complex protocols.
    *   **LLM Dependence:** LLM dependence, and associated costs, could limit adoption.

*Score: 6*

**Justification:**

FLAG presents a valuable engineering contribution by effectively combining existing techniques to address a practical problem in hardware verification. The framework offers a promising approach to automate the generation of formal specifications for communication protocols, potentially improving design and verification productivity. However, the limited generalizability, the dependence on LLMs, and the remaining manual effort prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The incremental nature of the novelty, along with the practical limitations, justifies a score of 6.  While the approach shows good results within its niche, more work is required to broaden its applicability and reduce manual intervention to achieve a higher impact. Further studies that address scalability and complete automation would significantly strengthen the framework and justify a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Scene Perceived Image Perceptual Score (SPIPS): combining global and local perception for image quality assessment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17234v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiqiang Lao, Heather Yu
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of artificial intelligence and widespread use of smartphones have resulted in an exponential growth of image data, both real (camera-captured) and virtual (AI-generated). This surge underscores the critical need for robust image quality assessment (IQA) methods that accurately reflect human visual perception. Traditional IQA techniques primarily rely on spatial features - such as signal-to-noise ratio, local structural distortions, and texture inconsistencies - to identify artifacts. While effective for unprocessed or conventionally altered images, these methods fall short in the context of modern image post-processing powered by deep neural networks (DNNs). The rise of DNN-based models for image generation, enhancement, and restoration has significantly improved visual quality, yet made accurate assessment increasingly complex. To address this, we propose a novel IQA approach that bridges the gap between deep learning methods and human perception. Our model disentangles deep features into high-level semantic information and low-level perceptual details, treating each stream separately. These features are then combined with conventional IQA metrics to provide a more comprehensive evaluation framework. This hybrid design enables the model to assess both global context and intricate image details, better reflecting the human visual process, which first interprets overall structure before attending to fine-grained elements. The final stage employs a multilayer perceptron (MLP) to map the integrated features into a concise quality score. Experimental results demonstrate that our method achieves improved consistency with human perceptual judgments compared to existing IQA models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new full-reference image quality assessment (IQA) metric called Scene Perceived Image Perceptual Score (SPIPS). SPIPS aims to better align with human visual perception by combining deep learning features with traditional IQA metrics. The core idea is to disentangle deep features into high-level semantic information and low-level perceptual details, processing them separately before fusing them with metrics like PSNR, SSIM, and MS-SSIM. The model is trained and evaluated on the BAPPS dataset, demonstrating improved consistency with human perceptual judgments compared to existing IQA models. Ablation studies are conducted to assess the impact of individual components.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Hybrid Approach:** Combining deep learning features with traditional IQA metrics is a sensible approach. It leverages the strengths of both: deep learning for semantic understanding and traditional metrics for quantifiable distortions.
    * **Separation of Feature Streams:** Distinguishing between high-level semantic and low-level perceptual features mirrors the hierarchical nature of human vision and seems like a good design choice. Processing them separately might indeed give better results.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors perform a detailed evaluation against several established IQA metrics and conduct ablation studies to validate their design choices. This strengthens the claims made in the paper. Reporting category-wise performance is a plus.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and the methodology is clearly explained.  The use of figures to illustrate the model architecture and qualitative comparisons is effective.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the approach is sensible and well-executed, the novelty is somewhat incremental.  Other papers have also attempted to combine deep learning with traditional metrics. The primary distinction lies in the specific feature separation and fusion strategy, but this is not a fundamentally new concept.
    * **Reliance on Pre-trained Networks:** The model relies on pre-trained CNNs (AlexNet, VGG, SqueezeNet) as feature extractors.  While this is common practice, the choice of these specific networks is not particularly justified. More recent architectures specifically designed for visual representation learning could have been tested.  Moreover, the choice of early layers for "perceptual" features and later layers for "semantic" features might be overly simplistic and warrants a deeper exploration.
    * **Limited Generalizability Discussion:** While the results on BAPPS are encouraging, the BAPPS dataset has some limitations. The paper could have addressed the potential for overfitting to this specific dataset and discussed how the model might generalize to images with different types of distortions or content.
    * **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** The weights assigned to the traditional IQA and deep features components are determined empirically.  A more theoretical justification for these weights could further improve the model's performance.

* **Significance:** The paper contributes to the ongoing effort to develop IQA metrics that better reflect human perception. The results demonstrate improvements over existing methods, which is a valuable contribution. However, the limited novelty and reliance on established techniques lessen its overall significance. The paper could serve as a good case study for combining traditional and deep learning approaches.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper is unlikely to have a disruptive influence. However, it might inspire further research in the area of hybrid IQA metrics and encourage a more careful consideration of the different aspects of image quality (semantic vs. perceptual).

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a solid, well-executed approach to IQA. However, the novelty is relatively incremental.  The separation of features and fusion with traditional metrics is a sensible design, and the experiments demonstrate a clear improvement over existing methods on the BAPPS dataset. However, it relies on existing pretrained networks, and doesn't delve deeply into why the specific choices were made. Addressing the limitations regarding the dataset dependence and theoretical grounding would considerably enhance the paper's significance.

### NeuralGrok: Accelerate Grokking by Neural Gradient Transformation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17243v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyu Zhou, Simin Fan, Martin Jaggi, Jie Fu
- **Abstract**: Grokking is proposed and widely studied as an intricate phenomenon in which generalization is achieved after a long-lasting period of overfitting. In this work, we propose NeuralGrok, a novel gradient-based approach that learns an optimal gradient transformation to accelerate the generalization of transformers in arithmetic tasks. Specifically, NeuralGrok trains an auxiliary module (e.g., an MLP block) in conjunction with the base model. This module dynamically modulates the influence of individual gradient components based on their contribution to generalization, guided by a bilevel optimization algorithm. Our extensive experiments demonstrate that NeuralGrok significantly accelerates generalization, particularly in challenging arithmetic tasks. We also show that NeuralGrok promotes a more stable training paradigm, constantly reducing the model's complexity, while traditional regularization methods, such as weight decay, can introduce substantial instability and impede generalization. We further investigate the intrinsic model complexity leveraging a novel Absolute Gradient Entropy (AGE) metric, which explains that NeuralGrok effectively facilitates generalization by reducing the model complexity. We offer valuable insights on the grokking phenomenon of Transformer models, which encourages a deeper understanding of the fundamental principles governing generalization ability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Neural Grok," a novel gradient-based approach designed to accelerate the "grokking" phenomenon in transformer models, particularly in arithmetic tasks.  Grokking refers to the delayed generalization observed where models initially overfit to training data before suddenly generalizing to unseen data after prolonged training. Neural Grok uses an auxiliary module (neural-amplifier), trained via a bilevel optimization algorithm, to dynamically modulate the gradient components based on their contribution to generalization. Experimental results demonstrate that Neural Grok accelerates generalization on various arithmetic tasks, promotes more stable training, and reduces model complexity (measured using a new metric called Absolute Gradient Entropy (AGE)). The paper also contrasts Neural Grok with traditional regularization methods (like weight decay) and offers insights into the grokking phenomenon.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of learning a gradient transformation to accelerate grokking is novel. The use of a bilevel optimization framework for this purpose is also a significant contribution. Introducing Absolute Gradient Entropy (AGE) is a valuable attempt to quantify model complexity in the context of grokking. The comparison with standard regularization methods and the demonstration of their potential instability is also a novel observation.
* **Significance:**  The grokking phenomenon has attracted considerable attention, and any method that accelerates it has value.  Demonstrating more stable generalization through gradient transformation has significant implications. Understanding and controlling the grokking behavior could lead to more efficient training paradigms and robust models.  The AGE metric offers a new perspective for analyzing and understanding model complexity during training, which could be broadly applicable beyond just grokking.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem definition:**  The paper addresses a well-defined problem (accelerating grokking).
    * **Novel approach:** Neural Grok introduces a unique method for tackling the problem.
    * **Solid experimental results:**  Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of Neural Grok on diverse arithmetic tasks.
    * **Analysis of stability and complexity:** The analysis provides valuable insights into the dynamics of grokking and contrasts it with existing methods.
    * **Introduction of a new metric:**  Absolute Gradient Entropy (AGE) is a novel and potentially useful tool.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited scope of tasks:** The experiments are limited to arithmetic tasks. While these provide a controlled environment, it remains to be seen how well Neural Grok generalizes to more complex, real-world datasets and architectures (e.g., large language models).
    * **Limited transferability:** The gradient transformations exhibit low transferability across even similar arithmetic operations, suggesting that the learned transformations are highly task-specific, restricting its general usability.
    * **Bilevel optimization complexity:**  Bilevel optimization can be computationally expensive and challenging to tune. The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the practical challenges in implementing and scaling Neural Grok. The trade-off between added complexity and performance gain requires further clarification.
    * **Justification of hyperparameter selection:** The choice of particular model architecture for neural-amplifier is not clearly justified.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to accelerating grokking, supported by solid experimental evidence and insightful analysis. The introduction of the AGE metric is also a positive aspect. However, the limited scope of the experiments (arithmetic tasks only), low transferability, and the potential computational cost of bilevel optimization are important limitations.  The task-specific nature of the learned gradient transformations also reduces the method's broad applicability. The choice of using a MLP as the neural amplifier lacks justification. Overall, I'd assign it a score that reflects the paper's significance, while acknowledging its current limitations and need for future development.

### Low-Resource Neural Machine Translation Using Recurrent Neural Networks and Transfer Learning: A Case Study on English-to-Igbo
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17252v1)
- **Authors**: Ocheme Anthony Ekle, Biswarup Das
- **Abstract**: In this study, we develop Neural Machine Translation (NMT) and Transformer-based transfer learning models for English-to-Igbo translation - a low-resource African language spoken by over 40 million people across Nigeria and West Africa. Our models are trained on a curated and benchmarked dataset compiled from Bible corpora, local news, Wikipedia articles, and Common Crawl, all verified by native language experts. We leverage Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) architectures, including Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) and Gated Recurrent Units (GRU), enhanced with attention mechanisms to improve translation accuracy. To further enhance performance, we apply transfer learning using MarianNMT pre-trained models within the SimpleTransformers framework. Our RNN-based system achieves competitive results, closely matching existing English-Igbo benchmarks. With transfer learning, we observe a performance gain of +4.83 BLEU points, reaching an estimated translation accuracy of 70%. These findings highlight the effectiveness of combining RNNs with transfer learning to address the performance gap in low-resource language translation tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of low-resource machine translation (MT) by developing a Neural Machine Translation (NMT) system for English-to-Igbo. It combines RNN-based encoder-decoder models (specifically LSTM and GRU) with attention mechanisms.  The authors curate and benchmark a dataset for this task, explore different attention scoring functions, decoding strategies, and leverage transfer learning using a MarianNMT-based model. The experiments compare different architectures and hyperparameters, ultimately achieving a BLEU score of 0.43 with the transfer learning approach, exceeding existing benchmarks. The authors also demonstrate the generalizability of their approach by training on an English-French dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a significant problem:** Low-resource MT is a crucial area, particularly for underrepresented languages like Igbo. This work contributes directly to addressing this challenge.
    *   **Curated Dataset:** The authors curated and benchmarked a dataset which is a valuable contribution in itself. Igbo language NMT lacks publicly available and standardized datasets, making this effort beneficial.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper thoroughly explores several key aspects of NMT, including different RNN architectures (LSTM, GRU), attention mechanisms (dot-product, concat, general), decoding strategies, and hyperparameter tuning.
    *   **Transfer Learning Integration:** Effectively utilizes transfer learning through MarianNMT demonstrating significant performance gain in BLEU scores.
    *   **Generalizability:** Experiments on both Igbo and French demonstrates the applicability of the approach beyond a single language pair.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Data Size:** While a dataset was curated, ~12,000 sentence pairs in training is still considered small for deep learning models. Further performance gain could potentially be attained with larger training corpora.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The core architecture is based on well-established RNN-based encoder-decoder models with attention, The work is mostly an application of existing techniques and a comparison of hyperparameters and different methods, rather than introducing fundamentally novel components or algorithms. The primary contribution lies in applying these methods to a specific low-resource language.
    *   **Lack of In-Depth Linguistic Analysis:** While the paper benchmarks BLEU scores and discusses fluency, it lacks a deeper linguistic analysis of the generated translations. Error analysis and a comparison with human translations beyond BLEU scores would strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **Limited Transfer Learning:** only MarianNMT transfer learning model was investigated. Exploring other pretrained models for transfer learning in this domain could provide more comprehensive analysis.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a practical baseline for English-to-Igbo MT.
    *   The comparison of different techniques (RNN variants, attention mechanisms) offers valuable insights for researchers working on low-resource MT.
    *   The successful application of transfer learning highlights its importance in bridging the performance gap for under-resourced languages.
    *   The study's comprehensive approach provides practical guidance for building MT systems in other low-resource settings.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a solid engineering effort with comprehensive experimentation and valuable insights for low-resource machine translation. The curation and benchmarking of the data set is a notable contribution. The methodology adopted well-establishes techniques. However, the novelty is somewhat limited as the core approach relies on existing deep learning architectures. The study also could be improved with better linguistically analysis and more explorations in transfer learning techniques.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:** The score reflects the paper's practical contributions to a specific low-resource translation task (English-to-Igbo), the comprehensiveness of the experimentation, and the valuable insights gained. However, it is penalized for limited core algorithmic novelty and relative small data size in this day and age, and for less linguistic/error-based evaluation of the results beyond a BLEU score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### DIVE: Inverting Conditional Diffusion Models for Discriminative Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17253v1)
- **Authors**: Yinqi Li, Hong Chang, Ruibing Hou, Shiguang Shan, Xilin Chen
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have shown remarkable progress in various generative tasks such as image and video generation. This paper studies the problem of leveraging pretrained diffusion models for performing discriminative tasks. Specifically, we extend the discriminative capability of pretrained frozen generative diffusion models from the classification task to the more complex object detection task, by "inverting" a pretrained layout-to-image diffusion model. To this end, a gradient-based discrete optimization approach for replacing the heavy prediction enumeration process, and a prior distribution model for making more accurate use of the Bayes' rule, are proposed respectively. Empirical results show that this method is on par with basic discriminative object detection baselines on COCO dataset. In addition, our method can greatly speed up the previous diffusion-based method for classification without sacrificing accuracy. Code and models are available at https://github.com/LiYinqi/DIVE .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DIVE: Inverting Conditional Diffusion Models for Discriminative Tasks":

**Summary:**

The paper "DIVE: Inverting Conditional Diffusion Models for Discriminative Tasks" explores how pre-trained conditional diffusion models can be repurposed for discriminative tasks, specifically object detection. The authors propose a method called DIVE (Diffusion model InVErsion) that "inverts" a pre-trained layout-to-image diffusion model. Instead of generating images from labels, it finds the labels that best explain a given image.  DIVE uses a gradient-based discrete optimization to estimate the object labels (bounding boxes and classes). It further enhances the process by training a separate diffusion model to represent the prior distribution of object layouts, aiding the inversion process based on Bayes' rule.  The method demonstrates competitive results with basic object detectors on the COCO dataset and significantly speeds up a previous diffusion-based classification method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty (Strengths):** The core idea of directly inverting a pre-trained layout-to-image diffusion model for object detection is relatively novel.  Most prior work leverages generative models to either synthesize data for training discriminative models or to extract features for them. DIVE directly uses the generative model for prediction without training new components on real data. The discrete optimization approach, which allows gradients to be used on discrete embedding vocabularies, is a nice contribution. The use of a prior distribution model to refine the inversion process addresses a crucial issue: the unrealistic assumption of a uniform prior over object layouts.
*   **Novelty (Weaknesses):** While the concept is interesting, the technical contributions are incremental rather than revolutionary.  The gradient-based optimization approach is not new in the broader context of generative model inversion.  The optimization is also applied to a *discrete* variable domain using an approximate nearest neighbor technique, which while useful, is not conceptually profound. The use of a prior model is also an intuitive and expected step given the challenges outlined.
*   **Significance (Strengths):** The paper's significance lies in demonstrating the potential of pre-trained generative models for more complex discriminative tasks than simple classification.  It highlights that diffusion models, with their ability to model complex data distributions, have inherent discriminative capabilities that can be exploited. The potential for future research exploring more efficient ways to exploit this inherent information. It also provides a useful evaluation metric for conditional diffusion models by measuring their accuracy at the inverse task.
*   **Significance (Weaknesses):** The results, while competitive with basic detectors, are not state-of-the-art. There is a considerable performance gap between DIVE and more advanced discriminative detectors or feature extraction based solutions (particularly those with SD rather than LDM-8 backbone). The computational cost of the method is very high, limiting its practical applicability in its current form. The dependence on a pre-trained layout-to-image model also limits its applicability to tasks where such models are available. Furthermore, because the method relies on inversion, it is vulnerable to reconstruction artifacts, which would likely hinder performance on especially complex images.
*   **Clarity and Reproducibility:** The paper is reasonably well-written and provides sufficient details for understanding the method. Code availability further improves reproducibility. However, exact implementation details are not completely explicit, requiring some interpretation of the original code.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to object detection using pre-trained diffusion models. However, the technical contributions are somewhat incremental, building upon existing generative model inversion techniques. The empirical results, while promising, are not state-of-the-art, and the computational cost is a significant limitation.

The paper demonstrates the potential of generative models to perform discriminative tasks and provides insights into their inherent capabilities. It opens the door to further research on more efficient and effective ways to exploit these models for object detection and other complex tasks.

### JurisCTC: Enhancing Legal Judgment Prediction via Cross-Domain Transfer and Contrastive Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17264v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaolu Kang, Hongtian Cai, Xiangyang Ji, Jinzhe Li, Nanfei Gu
- **Abstract**: In recent years, Unsupervised Domain Adaptation (UDA) has gained significant attention in the field of Natural Language Processing (NLP) owing to its ability to enhance model generalization across diverse domains. However, its application for knowledge transfer between distinct legal domains remains largely unexplored. To address the challenges posed by lengthy and complex legal texts and the limited availability of large-scale annotated datasets, we propose JurisCTC, a novel model designed to improve the accuracy of Legal Judgment Prediction (LJP) tasks. Unlike existing approaches, JurisCTC facilitates effective knowledge transfer across various legal domains and employs contrastive learning to distinguish samples from different domains. Specifically, for the LJP task, we enable knowledge transfer between civil and criminal law domains. Compared to other models and specific large language models (LLMs), JurisCTC demonstrates notable advancements, achieving peak accuracies of 76.59% and 78.83%, respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the JurisCTC paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces JurisCTC, a novel model for Legal Judgment Prediction (LJP) that leverages unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) and contrastive learning to improve accuracy.  It aims to address the challenge of limited criminal law data by transferring knowledge from the richer civil law domain. JurisCTC utilizes a BERT feature extractor, a class classifier, and a domain classifier, incorporating cross-entropy loss, Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) loss, and contrastive loss to facilitate domain-invariant feature learning. The experiments demonstrate JurisCTC's superior performance compared to existing baselines and large language models (LLMs) in both criminal and civil law tasks, highlighting its effectiveness in cross-domain transfer and overall prediction accuracy. Ablation studies validate the contribution of each component of JurisCTC.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonable level of novelty. While UDA and contrastive learning are not new concepts, their specific application to cross-domain LJP between civil and criminal law is a valuable contribution. The architecture of JurisCTC, combining these techniques with BERT, is also a novel aspect. The paper highlights the crucial and practical contribution of a knowledge transfer model for dealing with annotated texts in the legal field and can achieve the transfer of knowledge from different departmental laws.

* **Significance:**  The significance of the paper is noteworthy. Addressing the data scarcity issue in criminal law LJP is a practical problem with real-world implications. Demonstrating that knowledge can be effectively transferred from civil law to improve criminal law prediction accuracy is a valuable finding. The improvement over strong baselines, including LLMs like Gemini and GPT, is substantial. The practical value of the model is highlighted, reducing legal costs and improving access to justice.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Problem Relevance:** Addresses a significant real-world problem in LJP related to data scarcity.
    * **Technical Soundness:** The model architecture is well-defined, and the use of BERT, UDA, contrastive learning is justified.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:**  Extensive experiments including comparisons against strong baselines and ablation studies provide strong evidence for the model's effectiveness.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Domain Exploration:** While the paper focuses on civil and criminal law, exploring transfer from more disparate legal domains could further demonstrate the model's generalizability.
    * **Ablation Study Detail:** While the ablation study is present, a more in-depth analysis of *why* each component contributes the way it does could be beneficial.  For example, is contrastive loss more effective for certain types of cases?
    * **Error Analysis:** The paper lacks a detailed error analysis. Understanding the types of cases where JurisCTC still fails could provide insights for future improvements.
    * **LLM Fine-Tuning Detail:** While mentioning comparing against LLMs, specific tuning strategies used for LLMs are not mentioned which could affect the reliability of the comparison.

* **Impact:** JurisCTC has the potential to influence the field of LJP by providing a practical solution for data scarcity issues. It could serve as a baseline for future research in cross-domain LJP. This research also sets a new theoretical standard for legal knowledge mining, and has important practical significance for guiding judicial practice.

* **Rigorous Rationale:** JurisCTC contributes a practical and effective approach to the important problem of data scarcity in LJP. It demonstrates a clear improvement over strong baselines, indicating the effectiveness of its architecture and training methodology. However, the weaknesses outlined above prevent it from achieving a higher score. Deeper investigation into the ablation studies, a more detailed error analysis, and more exploration of domain transfer would increase the paper's significance.

### Towards Generalized and Training-Free Text-Guided Semantic Manipulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17269v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Hong, Xiao Cai, Pengpeng Zeng, Shuai Zhang, Jingkuan Song, Lianli Gao, Heng Tao Shen
- **Abstract**: Text-guided semantic manipulation refers to semantically editing an image generated from a source prompt to match a target prompt, enabling the desired semantic changes (e.g., addition, removal, and style transfer) while preserving irrelevant contents. With the powerful generative capabilities of the diffusion model, the task has shown the potential to generate high-fidelity visual content. Nevertheless, existing methods either typically require time-consuming fine-tuning (inefficient), fail to accomplish multiple semantic manipulations (poorly extensible), and/or lack support for different modality tasks (limited generalizability). Upon further investigation, we find that the geometric properties of noises in the diffusion model are strongly correlated with the semantic changes. Motivated by this, we propose a novel $\textit{GTF}$ for text-guided semantic manipulation, which has the following attractive capabilities: 1) $\textbf{Generalized}$: our $\textit{GTF}$ supports multiple semantic manipulations (e.g., addition, removal, and style transfer) and can be seamlessly integrated into all diffusion-based methods (i.e., Plug-and-play) across different modalities (i.e., modality-agnostic); and 2) $\textbf{Training-free}$: $\textit{GTF}$ produces high-fidelity results via simply controlling the geometric relationship between noises without tuning or optimization. Our extensive experiments demonstrate the efficacy of our approach, highlighting its potential to advance the state-of-the-art in semantics manipulation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces a "Generalized and Training-Free" (GTF) framework for text-guided semantic manipulation of images, videos, and 3D scenes.  Unlike existing methods, GTF aims to be modality-agnostic and doesn't require task-specific training or fine-tuning. The core idea revolves around manipulating the noise vectors in diffusion models. The authors observe that these noise vectors inherently encode semantic directions. By strategically combining noise predictions from source and target prompts (based on semantic addition or removal operations), GTF can guide the diffusion process to achieve the desired semantic transformations while preserving irrelevant content. The authors demonstrate their approach on various tasks including addition, removal, and style transfer in images, videos and 3D scenes, and present qualitative and quantitative comparisons against state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation**

* **Novelty:** The idea of directly manipulating noise in diffusion models for semantic manipulation isn't entirely new.  Prior work has explored noise manipulation or prompt interpolation.  However, the strength of this paper lies in its methodical analysis of noise semantics and the resulting framework (GTF) which supports multiple types of semantic operations and is broadly applicable across different modalities *without* training. The method's claim to be completely training-free and modality-agnostic does represent a substantial improvement in terms of ease of use and generalizability.
* **Significance:** The paper provides a compelling step towards unified and user-friendly semantic editing framework. By removing the need for training or fine-tuning for each specific modality or task, GTF lowers the barrier to entry and makes semantic manipulation more accessible. The consistent gains across image, video, and 3D editing tasks highlight the potential of the framework. The paper highlights an important direction for future research in generative models by better understanding and leveraging the underlying representations.

**Strengths:**

*   **Modality Agnostic:** The key strength of the work lies in the generalizability of the proposed GTF framework. Its ability to operate seamlessly across images, videos, and 3D scenes is a significant advantage.
*   **Training-Free:** Avoiding the need for training or fine-tuning makes the method significantly more practical and efficient.
*   **Strong Results:** The qualitative results are convincing, demonstrating precise and powerful semantic manipulation while preserving visual consistency. The quantitative results generally support these observations and demonstrate that GTF outperform SOTA methods in most cases.
*   **Principled Approach:** Deriving noise manipulation strategies from first principles based on Bayesian inference provides a solid theoretical grounding to the approach.
*   **Clear Explanation:** The paper provides clear and concise explanation of the underlying concepts behind GTF.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** While the paper touts the advantage of being training-free, it doesn't offer much discussion of the computational costs. Performing separate unconditional and conditional denoising processes and the noise manipulation steps could potentially be slow, especially for videos and 3D scenes.
*   **Parameter Sensitivity:**  The ablation study indicates that the method is sensitive to the choice of weights. Choosing suboptimal weights could negatively affect performance. How to choose the optimal weights for different scenarios is not adequately addressed. The effectiveness of different weight schedulers are also only evaluated for 3D Editing task. The paper lacks rigorous experiment results on which w2 schedulers should be used in image and video manipulation tasks.
*   **Dependence on Diffusion Models:** As the method is built on top of diffusion models, it inherits the limitations of diffusion models such as sensitivity to prompt and dependency on the underlying architecture of the diffusion model.
*   **Quantatative Comparsions:** While the quantatitive comparsions are good, some of the CLIP metric differences (ex. style CLIPSim in Table 3) are so small that they may not be significant.

**Justification of Score:**

Overall, the paper introduces a compelling, highly practical approach to text-guided semantic manipulation. Its training-free nature and modality-agnostic design make it a valuable contribution. However, some limitations such as the sensitivity to hyperparameters, computational overhead, and inherited issues from diffusion models limit its potential impact.

### Combining Static and Dynamic Approaches for Mining and Testing Constraints for RESTful API Testing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17287v1)
- **Authors**: Hieu Huynh, Tri Le, Vu Nguyen, Tien N. Nguyen
- **Abstract**: In API testing, deriving logical constraints on API response bodies is crucial in generating the test cases to cover various aspects of RESTful APIs. However, existing approaches are limited to dynamic analysis in which constraints are extracted from the execution of APIs as part of the system under test. The key limitation of such a dynamic approach is its under-estimation in which inputs in API executions are not sufficiently diverse to uncover actual constraints on API response bodies. In this paper, we propose to combine a novel static analysis approach (in which the constraints for API response bodies are mined from API specifications), with the dynamic approach (which relies on API execution data). We leverage large language models (LLMs) to comprehend the API specifications, mine constraints for response bodies, and generate test cases. To reduce LLMs' hallucination, we apply an Observation-Confirmation (OC) scheme which uses initial prompts to contextualize constraints. %, allowing subsequent prompts to more accurately confirm their presence. Our empirical results show that~LLMs with OC prompting achieve high precision in constraint mining with the average of 91.2%. When combining static and dynamic analysis, our tool, RBCTest , achieves a precision of 78.5%. RBCTest detects 107 constraints that the dynamic approach misses and 46 more precise constraints. We also use its generated test cases to detect 21 mismatches between the API specification and actual response data for 8 real-world APIs. Four of the mismatches were, in fact, reported in developers' forums.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RBCTEST, a novel approach for mining and testing constraints on API response bodies, addressing a critical need in RESTful API testing.  It combines static analysis (extracting constraints from API specifications using Large Language Models - LLMs) with dynamic analysis (leveraging API execution data, similar to AGORA).  The static analysis uses a novel Observation-Confirmation (OC) prompting scheme to reduce LLM hallucinations. The combined approach aims to overcome the limitations of purely dynamic analysis (which can under-estimate constraints due to insufficient input diversity) and offer broader coverage even when only the API specification or execution data is available.  The paper presents empirical results demonstrating that the LLM with OC prompting achieves high precision in constraint mining. RBCTEST improves over the dynamic approach by detecting more constraints and detecting mismatches between API specifications and actual response data, even uncovering previously reported issues in developer forums. The key contributions include the RBCTEST tool itself, a manually-verified benchmark for API response bodies, and an extensive evaluation demonstrating RBCTEST's superiority over baseline approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant problem in API testing:  the need for deriving logical constraints on API response bodies beyond simple status code and schema validation. This area is crucial for ensuring the reliability and correctness of API-driven applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty in Approach:** The combination of LLM-based static analysis and dynamic analysis is a significant contribution.  The OC prompting scheme specifically addresses the challenge of LLM hallucinations, which is a recognized problem in LLM-based applications. Using LLMs for API constraint mining *is* an emerging area, but the integration of dynamic analysis *alongside* a well-defined mitigation strategy for LLM issues *and* focusing explicitly on *response body constraints* makes this work distinct.

*   **Practical Significance:**  Detecting mismatches between API specifications and actual API behavior is crucial. The fact that RBCTEST detected issues reported in developer forums highlights its practical value.  Furthermore, the ability to function when *either* specification *or* execution data is available offers flexibility in different testing scenarios (e.g., early development, regression testing).

*   **Strong Evaluation:** The paper provides a rigorous evaluation using both a baseline dataset (AGORA) and a newly curated dataset with real-world APIs. The experiments are well-designed, with a clear methodology, appropriate metrics (precision, recall, F1-score), and analysis of the results. The inclusion of an ablation study (RQ5) further strengthens the evaluation by isolating the contributions of different components.

*   **Well-Structured and Clearly Written:** The paper is easy to follow, with a clear presentation of the problem, the proposed solution, the evaluation methodology, and the results. The motivating example effectively illustrates the limitations of existing approaches.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs:** While LLMs provide significant benefits, they also introduce uncertainty and potential for bias. The OC prompting mitigates hallucinations, but doesn't eliminate them entirely. The LLM's inherent knowledge and training data can influence the mined constraints, potentially leading to biases or limitations. There is no mention of specific prompt engineering techniques and what prompts were used. The actual prompts *are* provided in figures, but the lack of discussion on rationale is not good.

*   **Dynamic Analysis Integration:** While the combination of static and dynamic analysis is a strength, the paper could delve deeper into how the results from AGORA are effectively merged and prioritized with the LLM-mined constraints.  The "stricter condition" approach for resolving conflicts feels somewhat simplistic. More sophisticated integration strategies could be explored.

*   **Limited Types of Constraints:** The paper predominantly focuses on constraints related to data types, value ranges, and formats. More complex logical constraints or temporal constraints (e.g., constraints involving multiple API calls or state transitions) are not addressed in depth.

*   **Scalability:** The paper does not directly address the scalability of the approach. Running LLMs on large API specifications and with large volumes of execution data can be computationally expensive. This needs to be considered in future work.

*   **Threat to External Validity**: The paper covers a rather small number of services.

**Novelty and Significance Discussion:**

While using LLMs for API test case generation or specification augmentation is becoming increasingly common, RBCTEST's **specific contribution lies in the *combination* of static LLM-based constraint mining (with a dedicated hallucination mitigation strategy) *and* dynamic analysis to address response body constraints, offering a solution that adapts to different testing scenarios and overcomes limitations of solely static or dynamic techniques.** The validation of detected issues against developer forum reports adds significant weight to the practical impact of the work.

**Score:** 7.5/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a well-executed approach with strong empirical results and practical relevance. The novelty of combining static and dynamic analysis with specific LLM hallucination mitigation makes a valuable contribution to the field of API testing. However, the inherent limitations of LLMs, the simplified approach to merging static and dynamic results, the limited constraint types addressed, scalability concerns, and limited dataset size reduce the score. The overall contribution is substantial, but there are avenues for improvement and further research to fully realize the potential of the approach.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### AI-Enhanced Business Process Automation: A Case Study in the Insurance Domain Using Object-Centric Process Mining
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17295v1)
- **Authors**: Shahrzad Khayatbashi, Viktor Sjölind, Anders Granåker, Amin Jalali
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Artificial Intelligence (AI), particularly Large Language Models (LLMs), have enhanced organizations' ability to reengineer business processes by automating knowledge-intensive tasks. This automation drives digital transformation, often through gradual transitions that improve process efficiency and effectiveness. To fully assess the impact of such automation, a data-driven analysis approach is needed - one that examines how traditional and AI-enhanced process variants coexist during this transition. Object-Centric Process Mining (OCPM) has emerged as a valuable method that enables such analysis, yet real-world case studies are still needed to demonstrate its applicability.
