### LiveLongBench: Tackling Long-Context Understanding for Spoken Texts from Live Streams
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17366v1)
- **Authors**: Yongxuan Wu, Runyu Chen, Peiyu Liu, Hongjin Qian
- **Abstract**: Long-context understanding poses significant challenges in natural language processing, particularly for real-world dialogues characterized by speech-based elements, high redundancy, and uneven information density. Although large language models (LLMs) achieve impressive results on existing benchmarks, these datasets fail to reflect the complexities of such texts, limiting their applicability to practical scenarios. To bridge this gap, we construct the first spoken long-text dataset, derived from live streams, designed to reflect the redundancy-rich and conversational nature of real-world scenarios. We construct tasks in three categories: retrieval-dependent, reasoning-dependent, and hybrid. We then evaluate both popular LLMs and specialized methods to assess their ability to understand long-contexts in these tasks. Our results show that current methods exhibit strong task-specific preferences and perform poorly on highly redundant inputs, with no single method consistently outperforming others. We propose a new baseline that better handles redundancy in spoken text and achieves strong performance across tasks. Our findings highlight key limitations of current methods and suggest future directions for improving long-context understanding. Finally, our benchmark fills a gap in evaluating long-context spoken language understanding and provides a practical foundation for developing real-world e-commerce systems. The code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/Yarayx/livelongbench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LiveLongBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate long-context understanding capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) when processing spoken texts from live streams.  Recognizing that real-world spoken language presents unique challenges like high redundancy, informality, and uneven information density, the authors create a dataset derived from Douyin e-commerce live streams. The benchmark includes a variety of tasks spanning retrieval, reasoning, and hybrid question answering to assess how well LLMs and context compression methods can handle these complexities. The paper evaluates several popular LLMs and proposes a hybrid Key-Value (KV) cache compression strategy, combining different techniques, to improve performance and memory efficiency. The findings demonstrate that current methods struggle with long spoken contexts, particularly in the presence of high redundancy, and that the proposed hybrid compression strategy can offer superior performance-memory trade-offs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *specific application domain* (live-stream spoken text) and the construction of a benchmark tailored to the associated challenges. While long-context understanding and context compression are active research areas, previous benchmarks have largely focused on more formal, written texts. The creation of a bilingual benchmark focusing on spoken language with its inherent redundancies and informalities represents a significant contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLMs for real-world applications involving spoken language. The findings highlight the limitations of current LLMs when dealing with the characteristics of conversational speech and redundancy, providing valuable insights for researchers and practitioners. The exploration of hybrid compression strategies and the DEA analysis for optimizing performance and memory efficiency offer practical guidance for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments. This offers actionable insights to the field.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Targeted Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the specific challenges posed by long-context understanding in spoken language, particularly related to redundancy and informality.
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** LiveLongBench provides a well-designed and diverse set of tasks to evaluate various aspects of long-context understanding, including retrieval, reasoning, and hybrid capabilities. The inclusion of both closed and open-domain question types adds further robustness to the evaluation.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper conducts a systematic evaluation of several LLMs and context compression methods, providing a comprehensive overview of their strengths and weaknesses. The inclusion of both score and exact match as evaluation metrics provides a robust measure of performance.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The proposal of a hybrid KV cache compression strategy and the DEA analysis offer valuable guidance for improving the efficiency and effectiveness of LLMs in real-world applications.
    *   **Good experimental design:** The setup is clearly explained, the models and metrics are justified, and the conclusions are appropriately supported by the evidence.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** While the focus on live streams is a strength, it also introduces a limitation. The dataset may not fully represent the diversity of spoken language across different domains and contexts (e.g., academic lectures, customer service calls). The paper acknowledges this limitation.
    *   **Simplistic Baseline:** While the proposed hybrid compression strategy shows promise, it might be considered a relatively simplistic approach. More sophisticated or adaptive compression techniques could potentially achieve even better results.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The qualitative analysis or case studies in the supplementary materials would benefit from greater depth. While they provide some insight into the behavior of the models under different conditions, more elaborate examples could further illustrate the challenges.
    *   **Annotation cost/effort**: More discussion should be dedicated to the cost and amount of effort that it took to create the dataset.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence research in long-context understanding, particularly in the context of spoken language processing. It can also serve as a valuable resource for practitioners seeking to deploy LLMs in real-world applications.
*   **Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

I am assigning a score of 7.5 because the paper makes a valuable and novel contribution by introducing a benchmark specifically designed to address the challenges of long-context understanding in spoken language. The paper is well-written, the experiments are well-designed, and the results are thoroughly analyzed. However, the reliance on a limited dataset, the relative simplicity of the proposed compression strategy, and the annotation effort somewhat limit its impact. Despite these limitations, the paper offers valuable insights and practical guidance for improving the efficiency and effectiveness of LLMs in real-world applications involving spoken language. The contributions are significant, yet there's room for further improvement in expanding the dataset and exploring more advanced compression techniques. The LiveLongBench should become a standard method for testing LLMs in the future.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### On-Device Qwen2.5: Efficient LLM Inference with Model Compression and Hardware Acceleration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17376v1)
- **Authors**: Maoyang Xiang, Ramesh Fernando, Bo Wang
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly advanced AI capabilities but pose considerable challenges for deployment on edge devices due to high computational demands, memory bandwidth constraints, and energy consumption. This paper addresses these challenges by presenting an efficient framework for deploying the Qwen2.5-0.5B model on the Xilinx Kria KV260 edge platform, a heterogeneous system integrating an ARM Cortex-A53 CPU with reconfigurable FPGA logic. Leveraging Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) with FPGA-accelerated execution pipelines, the proposed approach enhances both model compression rate and system throughput. Additionally, we propose a hybrid execution strategy that intelligently offloads compute-intensive operations to the FPGA while utilizing the CPU for lighter tasks, effectively balancing the computational workload and maximizing overall performance. Our framework achieves a model compression rate of 55.08% compared to the original model and produces output at a rate of 5.1 tokens per second, outperforming the baseline performance of 2.8 tokens per second.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "On-Device Qwen2.5: Efficient LLM Inference with Model Compression and Hardware Acceleration" addresses the challenge of deploying large language models (LLMs) on resource-constrained edge devices. It proposes a framework for the Qwen2.5-0.5B model using Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) combined with FPGA-accelerated execution on a Xilinx Kria KV260 board. The framework uses a hybrid execution strategy, offloading computationally intensive tasks to the FPGA while utilizing the CPU for lighter tasks. The key results are a significant model compression rate and improved inference speed compared to a baseline implementation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Problem Addressed:** The paper tackles a relevant and important problem: deploying LLMs on edge devices. This is a growing area of interest due to the need for low-latency, privacy-preserving, and disconnected AI applications.
*   **Hybrid Approach:** The hybrid CPU/FPGA execution strategy is a good design choice, exploiting the strengths of each architecture.  This allows for balancing the computational load.
*   **Effective Compression:** Achieving a 55.1% compression rate using AWQ is a valuable contribution, allowing for smaller memory footprint without drastic performance degradation.
*   **Performance Improvement:** Demonstrating a significant speedup (5.1 tokens/second vs. 2.8 tokens/second) validates the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
*   **Clear Explanation:** The paper provides a clear explanation of the methodology, including the software optimizations (AWQ, data packing) and hardware design (MAC units, dataflow).
*   **Reproducible Experimentation:** The paper provided details of the hardware configuration (Xilinx Kria KV260) and the software implementation (based on C and using -Ofast optimization) facilitating future replications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** The paper combines existing techniques (AWQ, FPGA acceleration) rather than introducing truly novel algorithms or architectures. The novelty lies primarily in the specific application and integration of these methods.
*   **AWQ Parameter Tuning:** While the AWQ algorithm is known, more detail on how parameters were adjusted to improve model compression rate without incurring heavy accuracy loss would be welcomed.
*   **WNLI Benchmark:** The WNLI (Winograd Schema Challenge) benchmark is considered unreliable for evaluating language models due to its limited size and susceptibility to statistical biases. Using more robust and diverse benchmarks would provide a more convincing evaluation of the model's performance.
*   **Energy Consumption Comparison:** The paper lacks a quantitative comparison of power/energy consumption between the baseline and the proposed approach.  Edge deployment is often energy-sensitive, making this comparison crucial.
*   **FPGA-Specific Design:** The hardware accelerator is tailored to the Xilinx Kria KV260. Generalizing the accelerator for other FPGA platforms may be a more insightful contribution.
*   **Lack of comparison with SOTA techniques:** The paper lacks comparison with existing LLM techniques in edge devices, making it hard to know the actual improvement.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of work on efficient LLM inference on edge devices. While it doesn't present revolutionary new techniques, the effective combination and implementation of existing methods, resulting in tangible performance gains, is a valuable contribution. It provides a practical example of how to deploy a moderately sized LLM (Qwen2.5-0.5B) on a relatively accessible FPGA-based edge platform.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is practically relevant and presents a well-engineered solution to an important problem. However, the lack of strong algorithmic novelty, limited benchmark results, and missing energy consumption analysis weaken its overall contribution. While it adds to the engineering knowledge of deploying LLMs on FPGAs, it doesn't significantly push the boundaries of the field. The absence of comparison with state-of-the-art techniques makes it impossible to assess the contributions in a broader context.

- **Score**: 6/10

### Assessing the Capability of Large Language Models for Domain-Specific Ontology Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17402v1)
- **Authors**: Anna Sofia Lippolis, Mohammad Javad Saeedizade, Robin Keskisarkka, Aldo Gangemi, Eva Blomqvist, Andrea Giovanni Nuzzolese
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown significant potential for ontology engineering. However, it is still unclear to what extent they are applicable to the task of domain-specific ontology generation. In this study, we explore the application of LLMs for automated ontology generation and evaluate their performance across different domains. Specifically, we investigate the generalizability of two state-of-the-art LLMs, DeepSeek and o1-preview, both equipped with reasoning capabilities, by generating ontologies from a set of competency questions (CQs) and related user stories. Our experimental setup comprises six distinct domains carried out in existing ontology engineering projects and a total of 95 curated CQs designed to test the models' reasoning for ontology engineering. Our findings show that with both LLMs, the performance of the experiments is remarkably consistent across all domains, indicating that these methods are capable of generalizing ontology generation tasks irrespective of the domain. These results highlight the potential of LLM-based approaches in achieving scalable and domain-agnostic ontology construction and lay the groundwork for further research into enhancing automated reasoning and knowledge representation techniques.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of large language models (LLMs) to generate domain-specific ontologies automatically. It compares two state-of-the-art LLMs, DeepSeek and OpenAI's ol-preview (with reasoning capabilities), across six different knowledge domains. The LLMs are tasked with generating ontologies from competency questions (CQs) and associated user stories. The study finds that both LLMs exhibit consistent performance across domains, indicating their generalizability for ontology generation, irrespective of the domain. This suggests that LLMs are promising tools for scalable and domain-agnostic ontology construction.  The study also highlights a trade-off between the two models in terms of error types and demonstrates that the models are equally capable of handling easy and hard CQs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper builds upon previous work on LLM-based ontology generation but extends it in important ways. The primary novelty lies in the systematic evaluation of LLMs *across multiple, distinct domains* using a well-defined dataset of competency questions and user stories. Previous studies typically focused on single domains or a limited set of examples. The focus on *reasoning-based LLMs* and the careful crafting of prompts to leverage their capabilities are also incremental contributions. The explicit analysis of different error types (minor issues vs. complete failures) and their relation to specific domains (e.g., Events) adds another layer of granularity.

*   **Significance:**  The findings are significant because they demonstrate the potential of LLMs for automating a labor-intensive task in knowledge engineering. Domain-agnostic ontology generation could significantly reduce the cost and time associated with building ontologies for new applications. Showing that performance is consistent across various domains is crucial for practical adoption. The error analysis also provides valuable insights for improving LLM-based ontology generation methods. The investigation on prompt strategies is of relevance as well.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a well-defined experimental setup with a carefully curated dataset and a clear evaluation framework.
    *   **Domain Diversity:** The use of six distinct domains enhances the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** Comparing two different LLMs provides valuable insights into their relative strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The detailed error analysis helps identify areas for improvement in LLM-based ontology generation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Number of LLMs:** The study only evaluates two LLMs. While they are state-of-the-art, expanding the evaluation to a broader range of models (including open-source alternatives) would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Dataset Size:** While the dataset includes 95 CQs, a larger dataset might provide more statistically significant results.
    *   **Subjective Evaluation:** The classification of CQs into "Easy" and "Hard" categories, while validated, still involves some degree of subjectivity. A more objective metric (e.g., based on the complexity of SPARQL queries) would be beneficial.
    *   **Dataset Leakage Discussion:** While the paper acknowledges the risk of dataset leakage, the mitigation strategies could be strengthened.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    *   It provides a benchmark for evaluating LLM-based ontology generation methods.
    *   It highlights the importance of domain diversity in evaluation datasets.
    *   It identifies specific areas (e.g., error analysis) where further research is needed.
    *   It could motivate the development of new LLM-based tools and techniques for ontology engineering.

*   **Rigor of Rationale:** The paper's conclusions are generally well-supported by the experimental results. The discussion of limitations is appropriate and balanced. However, the degree to which "domain-agnostic" has been rigorously proven is somewhat overstated. The consistency *across* domains doesn't necessarily mean the performance is optimal *within* each domain. Further refinement of evaluation metrics would allow a more rigorous quantification of this 'domain agnostic' property.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed study that contributes to the growing field of LLM-based ontology engineering. The systematic evaluation across multiple domains, the comparative analysis of different LLMs, and the error analysis are all significant strengths. While the study has some limitations (e.g., the relatively small number of LLMs evaluated, some degree of subjective evaluation in CQ classification), the findings are compelling and have the potential to influence future research and development in this area. A higher score would be warranted with a larger-scale evaluation and more rigorous quantification of domain-agnosticism.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### 3DV-TON: Textured 3D-Guided Consistent Video Try-on via Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17414v1)
- **Authors**: Min Wei, Chaohui Yu, Jingkai Zhou, Fan Wang
- **Abstract**: Video try-on replaces clothing in videos with target garments. Existing methods struggle to generate high-quality and temporally consistent results when handling complex clothing patterns and diverse body poses. We present 3DV-TON, a novel diffusion-based framework for generating high-fidelity and temporally consistent video try-on results. Our approach employs generated animatable textured 3D meshes as explicit frame-level guidance, alleviating the issue of models over-focusing on appearance fidelity at the expanse of motion coherence. This is achieved by enabling direct reference to consistent garment texture movements throughout video sequences. The proposed method features an adaptive pipeline for generating dynamic 3D guidance: (1) selecting a keyframe for initial 2D image try-on, followed by (2) reconstructing and animating a textured 3D mesh synchronized with original video poses. We further introduce a robust rectangular masking strategy that successfully mitigates artifact propagation caused by leaking clothing information during dynamic human and garment movements. To advance video try-on research, we introduce HR-VVT, a high-resolution benchmark dataset containing 130 videos with diverse clothing types and scenarios. Quantitative and qualitative results demonstrate our superior performance over existing methods. The project page is at this link https://2y7c3.github.io/3DV-TON/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the 3DV-TON paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "3DV-TON: Textured 3D-Guided Consistent Video Try-on via Diffusion Models" introduces a novel framework for video try-on that leverages textured 3D guidance to improve the temporal consistency and visual quality of generated videos.  Instead of relying solely on pixel-based reconstruction losses common in diffusion models, the method uses animatable textured 3D meshes of humans wearing the target clothing. This explicit 3D guidance ensures that the garment's texture and shape remain consistent across different poses and viewpoints in the video.  The method includes a pipeline for generating dynamic 3D guidance from a keyframe image try-on, a rectangular masking strategy to prevent clothing information leakage, and a diffusion-based architecture for image synthesis. The paper also introduces a new high-resolution video try-on dataset (HR-VVT) for benchmarking.  The results demonstrate that 3DV-TON outperforms existing methods in both quantitative and qualitative evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty lies primarily in the use of *textured* 3D guidance within a diffusion-based video try-on framework. Existing methods either rely on warping, geometric 3D priors alone, or pixel-based reconstruction losses which often struggle with temporal consistency. By using an *explicit, textured* 3D mesh that is animated in sync with the video, the model gains a much stronger constraint on the shape and appearance of the clothing throughout the sequence. This is a significant step forward. The adaptive 3D mesh creation pipeline and the masking strategy are also worthwhile contributions that address practical challenges. The new HR-VVT dataset is a valuable resource for the community, addressing the limitations of existing benchmarks.

**Significance:**

The paper tackles a significant problem in video try-on: maintaining temporal consistency and visual fidelity while handling complex motions and clothing deformations. The demonstrated improvements are substantial, leading to more realistic and usable video try-on experiences.  The introduction of the HR-VVT dataset is also important as it provides a more challenging benchmark for evaluating future research.  The method's ability to handle diverse clothing styles and body poses is a step towards making video try-on a more practical technology for e-commerce and other applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem statement and well-defined solution:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing video try-on methods and proposes a targeted solution based on textured 3D guidance.
*   **Solid technical approach:** The method is well-engineered, combining diffusion models with 3D mesh reconstruction and animation techniques. The pipeline for generating the 3D guidance is reasonably complex.
*   **Strong experimental results:**  Both qualitative and quantitative results demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method over existing approaches. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the effectiveness of different components.
*   **Valuable dataset:** The introduction of the HR-VVT dataset addresses a recognized need for more challenging and high-resolution benchmarks in the field.
*   **Good writing and presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the technical details of the proposed method. The figures and tables are informative and effectively illustrate the results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational cost:** The 3D mesh reconstruction step is computationally expensive. While the paper mentions optimizations, the reconstruction time could still be a bottleneck in some applications. While the improved feedforward clothed human reconstruction may address this moving forward, it does represent a weakness of the current method.
*   **Dependence on HPS regressors:** Performance depends on the quality of initial human pose and shape estimates from HPS regressors which may not be perfectly accurate. Reliance on external libraries and data-driven HPS can introduce bias or errors.
*   **Masking artifacts:** While the masking strategy helps to prevent clothing leakage, it might also introduce artifacts or limit the ability to generate highly detailed or intricate clothing styles.
*   **Potential for negative societal impact:** Like all video generation technologies, there is a potential for misuse, such as the creation of deepfakes or the spread of misinformation.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field of video try-on. The idea of using textured 3D guidance as a strong constraint for temporal consistency is a valuable contribution that could be adopted by other researchers. The HR-VVT dataset will likely become a standard benchmark for evaluating future video try-on methods. Additionally, the pipeline developed will have applications to additional video generation projects.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid technical contribution with significant improvements over existing methods. The textured 3D guidance is a novel and effective way to address the problem of temporal inconsistency in video try-on. The HR-VVT dataset is a valuable resource for the community. While there are some limitations (computational cost, dependence on HPS regressors, potential masking artifacts), the overall impact of the paper is positive.

### Towards Harnessing the Collaborative Power of Large and Small Models for Domain Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17421v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Liu, Bingjie Yan, Tianyuan Zou, Jianqing Zhang, Zixuan Gu, Jianbing Ding, Xidong Wang, Jingyi Li, Xiaozhou Ye, Ye Ouyang, Qiang Yang, Ya-Qin Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities, but they require vast amounts of data and computational resources. In contrast, smaller models (SMs), while less powerful, can be more efficient and tailored to specific domains. In this position paper, we argue that taking a collaborative approach, where large and small models work synergistically, can accelerate the adaptation of LLMs to private domains and unlock new potential in AI. We explore various strategies for model collaboration and identify potential challenges and opportunities. Building upon this, we advocate for industry-driven research that prioritizes multi-objective benchmarks on real-world private datasets and applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, with a score and justification:

**Summary:**

This paper is a position paper advocating for a collaborative approach between large language models (LLMs) and smaller models (SMs) to address challenges in private domain tasks. It argues that LLMs, while powerful, are resource-intensive and raise privacy concerns when applied to sensitive data. SMs, on the other hand, are efficient and can operate on edge devices, but lack the broad knowledge of LLMs. The paper proposes a collaborative framework where LLMs and SMs work synergistically, exploring various knowledge transfer strategies, including distillation, generation-based approaches, and parameter-efficient tuning. It identifies key challenges such as data privacy, model security, communication costs, and the need for standardized benchmarks. Finally, it suggests future directions, emphasizing application-driven research with multi-objective benchmarks on real-world private datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper doesn't present radically novel *techniques* themselves. It synthesizes existing knowledge transfer methods from LLMs to SMs and vice versa, highlighting their potential in collaborative settings. The novelty lies primarily in its *framing* of the problem – explicitly articulating the need for and benefits of collaboration between LLMs and SMs within the specific context of data privacy and resource constraints in private domains. While each technique has been touched on separately, the clear and direct framing of this problem makes it relatively novel in how it brings different approaches together.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its practical relevance. Data privacy is a major impediment to deploying LLMs in many real-world scenarios, especially within businesses and government institutions. By advocating for a collaborative approach with SMs, the paper points to a viable path for harnessing the power of LLMs while mitigating privacy risks and resource limitations. The call for industry-driven research and standardized benchmarks is also valuable for guiding future research efforts and facilitating the adoption of collaborative AI systems. The paper's overview of existing transfer methods provides a helpful organization of the space. This increases accessibility and reduces the barrier to entry.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of adapting LLMs to private domains with privacy constraints and limited resources.

    *   **Comprehensive Overview:** It provides a decent overview of existing knowledge transfer techniques and their potential applications in the collaborative framework.

    *   **Emphasis on Practicality:** The paper's focus on application-driven research and multi-objective benchmarks highlights its concern for real-world deployment.

    *   **Well-Organized:** Its format makes this paper well-structured, improving accessibility and reading.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Specificity:** The paper remains relatively high-level and doesn't delve into the technical details of how different knowledge transfer strategies can be effectively combined in specific application scenarios. It would have been more compelling with specific examples or case studies.

    *   **Limited Technical Contribution:** While the paper provides a useful synthesis of existing techniques, it lacks original technical contributions.

    *   **Overlooks Certain Challenges:** It briefly mentions privacy threats but perhaps could explore model security threats more. While model security and intellectual property concerns are brought up, the implications and potential solutions might be elaborated on more.

    *   **The "kitchen sink" problem.** There are many different approaches, which are given equal (or at least seemingly similar) weights. This makes it difficult for readers to determine which is more important.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by encouraging the development of collaborative AI systems that address both the performance requirements and privacy concerns of private domain tasks. It could also stimulate the creation of new benchmarks and evaluation metrics that better reflect the complexities of real-world deployments.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning this paper a score of **6**.

*   It *does* offer a valuable perspective by articulating a need for collaboration and synthesizing existing knowledge transfer methods. It frames this collaboration to address privacy concerns for real-world businesses.
*   However, it does *not* introduce significant novel technical *ideas* or provide in-depth solutions to the identified challenges. It synthesizes existing ideas that have been individually published.
*   Its value lies primarily in its framing of the problem and its emphasis on practicality and standardized benchmarks. Because of its lack of specific solutions or original methods, the score is moderate.
    Score: 6

### Towards Leveraging Large Language Model Summaries for Topic Modeling in Source Code
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17426v1)
- **Authors**: Michele Carissimi, Martina Saletta, Claudio Ferretti
- **Abstract**: Understanding source code is a topic of great interest in the software engineering community, since it can help programmers in various tasks such as software maintenance and reuse. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable program comprehension capabilities, while transformer-based topic modeling techniques offer effective ways to extract semantic information from text. This paper proposes and explores a novel approach that combines these strengths to automatically identify meaningful topics in a corpus of Python programs. Our method consists in applying topic modeling on the descriptions obtained by asking an LLM to summarize the code. To assess the internal consistency of the extracted topics, we compare them against topics inferred from function names alone, and those derived from existing docstrings. Experimental results suggest that leveraging LLM-generated summaries provides interpretable and semantically rich representation of code structure. The promising results suggest that our approach can be fruitfully applied in various software engineering tasks such as automatic documentation and tagging, code search, software reorganization and knowledge discovery in large repositories.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Towards Leveraging Large Language Model Summaries for Topic Modeling in Source Code." Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel approach for topic modeling in source code by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate summaries of code functions. The method involves removing comments and obfuscating function names from Python code to remove any natural language cues.  Then, an LLM generates summaries of these functions. Finally, topic modeling is applied to the LLM-generated summaries. The authors compare the resulting topics with those derived from function names and docstrings to assess their internal consistency and semantic richness. The experimental results suggest that using LLM-generated summaries provides interpretable and semantically rich code representation. The authors claim that this approach can be useful in software engineering tasks like automatic documentation, code search, and software reorganization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs to generate summaries of obfuscated code specifically for topic modeling is relatively novel. Prior work in topic modeling of source code relies heavily on existing natural language elements (identifiers, comments, docstrings). The paper's core contribution is exploring whether the semantic understanding embedded within LLMs can be extracted via summarization to perform topic modeling in the absence of such natural language cues. This is a valuable exploration.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to analyze codebases where documentation is lacking or inconsistent, or where identifiers are poorly chosen. The ability to extract meaningful topics even without natural language elements could have practical implications for code understanding, maintenance, and reuse. The paper claims applications in automatic documentation and code search, which are very important tasks in software development.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly defines the problem they are trying to solve and why it's important.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The combination of LLM summarization and topic modeling for source code analysis is interesting and potentially useful.
    *   **Experimental Evaluation:** The paper presents a quantitative evaluation using metrics such as coherence scores, MSE, and topic overlap to compare their approach with baselines (docstrings and function names).
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The authors provide a qualitative analysis of the generated topics, which helps to understand the interpretability of the results. They also provide a clear overview of the experiment in Figure 1.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are limited to Python code and a single LLM (Gemma). It would be beneficial to see how the approach performs with different programming languages and LLMs.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** The paper mentions setting the number of topics to 40 after iterative evaluation, but doesn't provide much detail about the process or the criteria used to determine the "optimal" value. This makes it difficult to assess the robustness of the results.
    *   **Baseline Comparison:** While the paper compares with topics derived from docstrings and function names, a more comprehensive comparison with other established topic modeling techniques for source code (even those relying on natural language elements) would strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **Threats to Validity:** The evaluation compares the LLM-derived topics with topics derived from docstrings. This is reasonable as docstrings *should* reflect the code's purpose, but if the docstrings are poor, the comparison is less meaningful. The method also requires manual selection of the number of topics, which could bias the results.
    *   **Lack of Novel Metrics:** The metrics that are used aren't really specifically designed to gauge novelty, rather they capture how similar two sets of results are.
    *   **Practical Application:** Although some applications are claimed, there is no practical test of an application of their method.

*   **Potential Influence:** If validated on larger and more diverse codebases, this approach could become a valuable tool for software engineers. The technique could aid in reverse engineering, code understanding, and knowledge discovery. If the approach is automated and integrated into development environments, it could have a meaningful impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a relatively novel idea and provides a decent experimental evaluation. However, the limited scope, lack of detailed hyperparameter tuning, and somewhat superficial baseline comparison prevent it from being a truly outstanding contribution. While the results are promising, more rigorous validation and a more thorough comparison with existing techniques are needed to fully assess the potential impact of this approach. It provides a solid proof of concept and a foundation for further research, but the weaknesses need to be addressed before it can be considered a significant breakthrough.

- **Score**: 6/10

### Beyond Whole Dialogue Modeling: Contextual Disentanglement for Conversational Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17427v1)
- **Authors**: Guojia An, Jie Zou, Jiwei Wei, Chaoning Zhang, Fuming Sun, Yang Yang
- **Abstract**: Conversational recommender systems aim to provide personalized recommendations by analyzing and utilizing contextual information related to dialogue. However, existing methods typically model the dialogue context as a whole, neglecting the inherent complexity and entanglement within the dialogue. Specifically, a dialogue comprises both focus information and background information, which mutually influence each other. Current methods tend to model these two types of information mixedly, leading to misinterpretation of users' actual needs, thereby lowering the accuracy of recommendations. To address this issue, this paper proposes a novel model to introduce contextual disentanglement for improving conversational recommender systems, named DisenCRS. The proposed model DisenCRS employs a dual disentanglement framework, including self-supervised contrastive disentanglement and counterfactual inference disentanglement, to effectively distinguish focus information and background information from the dialogue context under unsupervised conditions. Moreover, we design an adaptive prompt learning module to automatically select the most suitable prompt based on the specific dialogue context, fully leveraging the power of large language models. Experimental results on two widely used public datasets demonstrate that DisenCRS significantly outperforms existing conversational recommendation models, achieving superior performance on both item recommendation and response generation tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel approach called DisenCRS (Contextual Disentanglement for Conversational Recommender System) to improve conversational recommender systems (CRS). DisenCRS tackles the issue of existing methods that model the entire dialogue context as a whole, neglecting the complex entanglement of focus and background information within the dialogue. It introduces a dual disentanglement framework comprised of self-supervised contrastive disentanglement and counterfactual inference disentanglement to effectively distinguish between these two types of information without supervision. Furthermore, it includes an adaptive prompt learning module that leverages large language models by selecting the most suitable prompt based on the specific dialogue context. Experiments on two benchmark datasets demonstrate that DisenCRS outperforms existing CRS models in both item recommendation and response generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in introducing contextual disentanglement to CRS. Previous methods often treat the dialogue context as a monolithic entity, while DisenCRS explicitly separates focus and background information. The combination of contrastive and counterfactual disentanglement in a self-supervised manner is also a noteworthy contribution. The adaptive prompt learning module, while building on existing prompt engineering techniques, offers a dynamic approach tailored to disentangled contexts.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve the accuracy and relevance of recommendations and responses in CRS. By disentangling focus and background information, DisenCRS aims to better capture user intent, leading to more effective recommendations and more coherent conversational interactions.  The performance gains on benchmark datasets, as reported in the paper, support this potential. This is significant because improving user experience is crucial for the broader adoption of CRS in various applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation. The paper effectively highlights the limitations of existing methods.
    *   Well-designed model architecture. The dual disentanglement framework and adaptive prompt learning module are logically connected and address the identified problem.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation. The experiments cover both recommendation and response generation tasks, and the comparison against strong baselines strengthens the claims. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of individual components.
    *   The use of self-supervision is a valuable aspect, as it removes the need for labeled data, which is often expensive and difficult to obtain in dialogue settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper assumes a clear distinction between "focus" and "background" information, which might not always be straightforward in real-world dialogues. The proxy signal used (entity information) might not be sufficient to capture this distinction perfectly. It would have been beneficial to include a qualitative analysis of examples where the disentanglement worked particularly well and where it failed.
    *   The adaptive prompt learning module, while beneficial, relies on a relatively simple prompt selection mechanism (MLP).  Exploring more advanced prompt selection techniques, such as those based on attention or reinforcement learning, could potentially further improve performance.
    *   The reliance on frozen DialoGPT limits the end-to-end optimization of the entire system. While freezing the LLM is a common practice, allowing for fine-tuning of at least a portion of the LLM could potentially lead to better results, though at a higher computational cost.
    *   The impact of different hyperparameters, particularly the disentanglement loss weight (λ), is investigated, but the paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the sensitivity of the model to these parameters and providing guidelines for setting them in different scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper introduces a valuable perspective on modeling dialogue context in CRS. It can potentially influence future research by:
    *   Encouraging the exploration of disentanglement techniques for various aspects of dialogue modeling beyond just focus and background information (e.g., user personality, emotional state).
    *   Inspiring the development of more sophisticated prompt learning methods tailored to disentangled contexts.
    *   Serving as a foundation for building more robust and adaptable CRS that can handle the complexities of real-world dialogues.

**Justification of Score:**

Overall, this paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of conversational recommender systems. The idea of disentangling focus and background information is well-motivated and effectively implemented. While the paper has some limitations, particularly in the assumptions made about the distinction between focus and background and the simplicity of the prompt selection mechanism, the comprehensive experimental results and the potential for future research outweigh these weaknesses. Therefore, I assign a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Breaking the Modality Barrier: Universal Embedding Learning with Multimodal LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17432v1)
- **Authors**: Tiancheng Gu, Kaicheng Yang, Ziyong Feng, Xingjun Wang, Yanzhao Zhang, Dingkun Long, Yingda Chen, Weidong Cai, Jiankang Deng
- **Abstract**: The Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) framework has become a widely used approach for multimodal representation learning, particularly in image-text retrieval and clustering. However, its efficacy is constrained by three key limitations: (1) text token truncation, (2) isolated image-text encoding, and (3) deficient compositionality due to bag-of-words behavior. While recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated significant advances in generalized vision-language understanding, their potential for learning transferable multimodal representations remains underexplored.In this work, we present UniME (Universal Multimodal Embedding), a novel two-stage framework that leverages MLLMs to learn discriminative representations for diverse downstream tasks. In the first stage, we perform textual discriminative knowledge distillation from a powerful LLM-based teacher model to enhance the embedding capability of the MLLM\'s language component. In the second stage, we introduce hard negative enhanced instruction tuning to further advance discriminative representation learning. Specifically, we initially mitigate false negative contamination and then sample multiple hard negatives per instance within each batch, forcing the model to focus on challenging samples. This approach not only improves discriminative power but also enhances instruction-following ability in downstream tasks. We conduct extensive experiments on the MMEB benchmark and multiple retrieval tasks, including short and long caption retrieval and compositional retrieval. Results demonstrate that UniME achieves consistent performance improvement across all tasks, exhibiting superior discriminative and compositional capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniME (Universal Multimodal Embedding), a novel two-stage framework for learning discriminative multimodal representations using Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The first stage involves textual discriminative knowledge distillation, where a powerful LLM-based teacher model guides the MLLM's language component. The second stage employs hard negative enhanced instruction tuning, which filters false negatives and samples multiple hard negatives to improve discriminative learning.  Experiments on MMEB and retrieval tasks demonstrate that UniME consistently improves performance, exhibiting superior discriminative and compositional capabilities. The paper argues that previous methods are limited by text token truncation, isolated image-text encoding, and deficient compositionality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has moderate novelty. While the idea of leveraging LLMs for multimodal representation learning isn't entirely new, the specific combination of textual discriminative knowledge distillation and hard negative enhanced instruction tuning within a two-stage framework is a contribution. E5-V and VLM2Vec are cited and compared to significantly. A crucial aspect is the claim of addressing limitations of CLIP (token truncation, isolated encoding, poor compositionality), but how effectively this is achieved is debatable given the continued reliance on MLLMs with their own inherent limitations. The hard negative sampling strategy is inspired by prior work, but the adaptation to the MLLM context, including false negative filtering, adds a valuable refinement.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to improve the performance of multimodal embedding models for various downstream tasks.  The extensive experimentation on the MMEB benchmark and retrieval tasks strengthens this claim. The claimed improvements in both discriminative and compositional capabilities are important. The focus on improving instruction following is also beneficial given its increasing importance in multimodal tasks. The practical usability of the framework given the reliance on large MLLMs is a potential limitation. However, the use of QLoRA makes fine-tuning more memory efficient, enhancing the broader accessibility of the model.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The two-stage framework offers a structured approach to multimodal representation learning.
    *   The hard negative enhanced instruction tuning strategy addresses a crucial issue in contrastive learning by mitigating false negative contamination.
    *   Extensive experimental evaluation on diverse benchmarks demonstrates consistent performance improvements.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the effectiveness of individual components of the framework.
    *   The visualizations offer qualitative understanding of the learned representations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on large MLLMs makes the framework computationally expensive. Although the work mentions QLoRA, it would have been stronger if they tested with smaller, faster MLLMs.
    *   While it attempts to overcome CLIP limitations, how the method deals with long text and complex queries should be explored.
    *   The novelty, while present, is incremental rather than revolutionary.
    *   The paper would benefit from a more detailed analysis of the failure cases.
    *   The paper needs to better emphasize the benefits of the components of the method.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research by encouraging exploration of different fine-tuning strategies to create enhanced methods.  The focus on compositional reasoning and instruction following may also drive innovation in these areas.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

A score of 6.5 reflects a paper with moderate novelty and significance. It leverages existing concepts (LLMs, contrastive learning, knowledge distillation, hard negative sampling) and combines them in a well-engineered framework with strong empirical validation. It does address certain limitations of existing methods (CLIP) and contributes a practical approach to multimodal embedding learning. The paper does not present a groundbreaking theoretical contribution or fundamentally shift the landscape of multimodal representation learning, the components of the method has been sufficiently validated.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Adaptive Orchestration of Modular Generative Information Access Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17454v1)
- **Authors**: Mohanna Hoveyda, Harrie Oosterhuis, Arjen P. de Vries, Maarten de Rijke, Faegheh Hasibi
- **Abstract**: Advancements in large language models (LLMs) have driven the emergence of complex new systems to provide access to information, that we will collectively refer to as modular generative information access (GenIA) systems. They integrate a broad and evolving range of specialized components, including LLMs, retrieval models, and a heterogeneous set of sources and tools. While modularity offers flexibility, it also raises critical challenges: How can we systematically characterize the space of possible modules and their interactions? How can we automate and optimize interactions among these heterogeneous components? And, how do we enable this modular system to dynamically adapt to varying user query requirements and evolving module capabilities? In this perspective paper, we argue that the architecture of future modular generative information access systems will not just assemble powerful components, but enable a self-organizing system through real-time adaptive orchestration -- where components' interactions are dynamically configured for each user input, maximizing information relevance while minimizing computational overhead. We give provisional answers to the questions raised above with a roadmap that depicts the key principles and methods for designing such an adaptive modular system. We identify pressing challenges, and propose avenues for addressing them in the years ahead. This perspective urges the IR community to rethink modular system designs for developing adaptive, self-optimizing, and future-ready architectures that evolve alongside their rapidly advancing underlying technologies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Adaptive Orchestration of Modular Generative Information Access Systems" proposes a novel architecture for future information retrieval (IR) systems, termed Generative Information Access (GenIA) systems.  The central argument is that GenIA systems should move beyond static pipelines and embrace dynamic, modular, and adaptive designs. The authors advocate for a system where components (modules) interact dynamically to construct customized pipelines based on user queries, module capabilities, and computational constraints.  They outline a framework based on graph representations, where nodes represent modules (tasks, agents, resources) and edges represent interactions.  The framework aims to optimize the effectiveness-efficiency trade-off of information retrieval through planning and reinforcement learning techniques. A specific instantiation of the framework using a contextual multi-armed bandit (CMAB) approach for a question-answering task is presented, demonstrating initial success in adapting to query complexity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adaptive orchestration in modular GenIA systems is indeed novel and addresses a critical limitation of current static pipeline approaches in IR. The proposed graph-based framework for representing and dynamically constructing pipelines is a valuable contribution. While modular architectures are not entirely new, the emphasis on *real-time adaptive* orchestration, where the entire pipeline structure is re-evaluated and optimized for each query, represents a significant advancement. The explicit consideration of an effectiveness-efficiency trade-off, driven by real-time feedback and adaptable to evolving modules, adds further novelty. It builds on existing work such as GPTSwarm, but introduces adaptivity in a way that static approaches lack.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to create more intelligent, efficient, and responsive information access systems.  As LLMs and other specialized components continue to evolve, the ability to dynamically adapt and incorporate these advancements is crucial. The paper highlights the limitations of monolithic LLMs and pre-defined pipelines in handling the diversity and complexity of user queries. By proposing a framework that learns and adapts over time, the authors pave the way for more personalized and resource-efficient information retrieval. The presented CMAB-based instantiation provides a concrete example demonstrating the feasibility and benefits of the proposed approach.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of inflexibility in existing IR architectures and the need for adaptive systems.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The proposed graph-based framework provides a solid foundation for implementing adaptive orchestration.
    *   **Comprehensive Module Categorization:** The breakdown of modules into tasks, agents, and resources is logical and helpful.
    *   **Concrete Instantiation:** The CMAB-based QA system demonstrates the practical application of the framework.
    *   **Consideration of Efficiency:** The emphasis on the effectiveness-efficiency trade-off and cost considerations is important and often overlooked in research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** While the CMAB instantiation is promising, the evaluation is relatively limited. The use of only Flan-T5 XL as the agent may not sufficiently capture the potential of the broader system architecture. A larger evaluation on more complex datasets and with diverse components is required. The approach is a first implementation on a very controlled data set, which lacks scalability and validation.
    *   **CMAB Implementation Details:** Some parts of CMAB implementation seems vague and lacks more details (reward).
    *   **Reliance on Labeled Data:** The CMAB approach relies on complexity labels in the question-answering dataset, which may not be available in all scenarios. The paper needs to address how to estimate query complexity without explicit labels.
    *   **Computational Complexity:** The potential computational overhead of dynamically constructing pipelines for each query is a concern that needs to be further addressed. While the framework aims to optimize for efficiency, the cost of the planning/RL process itself needs to be carefully considered.
    *   **Limited Implementation of other components:** The framework requires various components, but in the QA example, only a few components are tested. More experiments are expected.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence future research in IR and conversational AI. It provides a roadmap for developing adaptive, self-optimizing, and future-ready GenIA systems. It also opens up new research directions in module interaction, dynamic orchestration, and the use of planning/RL techniques in IR. Its greatest influence will be in inspiring more adaptive, efficient GenIA system designs.

* **Rigorous Rationale:** While the core idea and framework definition are valuable, there are significant limitations in the experimental evaluation and exploration of other aspects of framework, for example lack of a broader range of model testing (only one) and specific explanation of the framework modules. The experimental setting seems very controlled and does not provide a robust justification for the framework and its various characteristics.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Unified Attacks to Large Language Model Watermarks: Spoofing and Scrubbing in Unauthorized Knowledge Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17480v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Yi, Shunfan Zhengc, Linlin Wanga, Xiaoling Wang, Liang He
- **Abstract**: Watermarking has emerged as a critical technique for combating misinformation and protecting intellectual property in large language models (LLMs). A recent discovery, termed watermark radioactivity, reveals that watermarks embedded in teacher models can be inherited by student models through knowledge distillation. On the positive side, this inheritance allows for the detection of unauthorized knowledge distillation by identifying watermark traces in student models. However, the robustness of watermarks against scrubbing attacks and their unforgeability in the face of spoofing attacks under unauthorized knowledge distillation remain largely unexplored. Existing watermark attack methods either assume access to model internals or fail to simultaneously support both scrubbing and spoofing attacks. In this work, we propose Contrastive Decoding-Guided Knowledge Distillation (CDG-KD), a unified framework that enables bidirectional attacks under unauthorized knowledge distillation. Our approach employs contrastive decoding to extract corrupted or amplified watermark texts via comparing outputs from the student model and weakly watermarked references, followed by bidirectional distillation to train new student models capable of watermark removal and watermark forgery, respectively. Extensive experiments show that CDG-KD effectively performs attacks while preserving the general performance of the distilled model. Our findings underscore critical need for developing watermarking schemes that are robust and unforgeable.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes Contrastive Decoding-Guided Knowledge Distillation (CDG-KD), a novel framework for attacking watermark schemes in large language models (LLMs) under unauthorized knowledge distillation scenarios. CDG-KD enables both watermark scrubbing (removal) and spoofing (forgery) attacks by leveraging contrastive decoding to extract corrupted or amplified watermark text from a student model and a weakly watermarked reference model. This data is then used for bidirectional distillation to train new student models capable of either removing or forging watermarks.  The method operates entirely in a black-box setting, requiring no access to internal model parameters or modifications to the generation process. Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of CDG-KD in performing both types of attacks while preserving the general performance of the distilled model, highlighting vulnerabilities in current watermarking schemes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel, unified framework for conducting both scrubbing and spoofing attacks on watermarked LLMs under unauthorized knowledge distillation. Existing attack methods typically focus on one type of attack or require access to model internals. CDG-KD's black-box approach and bidirectional distillation strategy represent a significant advance in this area. The use of contrastive decoding to manipulate watermark strength is also a novel aspect. The exploration of idiosyncrasies of watermarking schemes, and its impact on detectability is an interesting contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a critical vulnerability in existing LLM watermarking schemes: their susceptibility to indirect attacks through unauthorized knowledge distillation. Watermark radioactivity, while intended as a detection mechanism for illicit model copying, can be exploited by adversaries to undermine the integrity of the watermark, enabling both removal and forgery. The paper's findings underscore the urgent need for more robust and unforgeable watermarking techniques. The practical demonstration of attacks in a black-box setting makes the threat very real and immediate.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The framework is unified, enabling both scrubbing and spoofing attacks.
    *   It operates in a black-box setting, making it highly practical.
    *   Contrastive decoding is an effective technique for manipulating watermark strength.
    *   Experiments demonstrate strong attack performance while preserving model utility.
    *   The paper clearly articulates the threat model and potential consequences.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method relies on access to a student model already distilled from a teacher model, which may not always be the case in real-world scenarios. However, it seems realistic assumption as this is how the adversaries will try to save computation costs by leveraging distillation on a larger model.
    *   The effectiveness of the attack is tied to the size and quality of the distillation corpus.
    *   The generalizability to different watermarking paradigms, particularly sentence-level schemes, is not fully explored. The experiments focuses mostly on token-level watermarking strategy.
    *   Ethical considerations are briefly mentioned, but a more in-depth discussion of the potential for misuse of the framework would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of LLM watermarking. It exposes a serious vulnerability that must be addressed to ensure the trustworthiness and accountability of these models. The CDG-KD framework provides a valuable tool for evaluating the robustness of existing and future watermarking schemes. The research will drive further research into more resistant and unforgeable watermarking methods. The ability to measure watermark detectability as a classification problem is a useful additional finding.

**Justification for Score:**

This is a well-executed and significant piece of research. It makes a clear contribution by exposing a real and practical attack vector against LLM watermarking. The unified approach, black-box setting, and experimental validation make a strong case for its importance. While the method has some limitations (primarily related to the reliance on a distilled student model and dataset size), these do not detract significantly from its overall value.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Combining GCN Structural Learning with LLM Chemical Knowledge for or Enhanced Virtual Screening
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17497v1)
- **Authors**: Radia Berreziga, Mohammed Brahimi, Khairedine Kraim, Hamid Azzoune
- **Abstract**: Virtual screening plays a critical role in modern drug discovery by enabling the identification of promising candidate molecules for experimental validation. Traditional machine learning methods such as support vector machines (SVM) and XGBoost rely on predefined molecular representations, often leading to information loss and potential bias. In contrast, deep learning approaches-particularly Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs)-offer a more expressive and unbiased alternative by operating directly on molecular graphs. Meanwhile, Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently demonstrated state-of-the-art performance in drug design, thanks to their capacity to capture complex chemical patterns from large-scale data via attention mechanisms. In this paper, we propose a hybrid architecture that integrates GCNs with LLM-derived embeddings to combine localized structural learning with global chemical knowledge. The LLM embeddings can be precomputed and stored in a molecular feature library, removing the need to rerun the LLM during training or inference and thus maintaining computational efficiency. We found that concatenating the LLM embeddings after each GCN layer-rather than only at the final layer-significantly improves performance, enabling deeper integration of global context throughout the network. The resulting model achieves superior results, with an F1-score of (88.8%), outperforming standalone GCN (87.9%), XGBoost (85.5%), and SVM (85.4%) baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel hybrid architecture for virtual screening that combines Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) with Large Language Model (LLM)-derived embeddings of SMILES strings.  The key innovation is integrating pre-trained LLM embeddings at each layer of the GCN, allowing the model to combine localized structural learning with global chemical knowledge. The authors demonstrate that this approach outperforms standalone GCNs, XGBoost, and SVM baselines in molecular classification tasks, achieving superior F1-scores and AUC-ROC values across several datasets. They attribute the improved performance to the richer representation learned by integrating textual and graphical information at multiple stages of the GCN.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novelty:** The core idea of fusing LLM-derived chemical knowledge with GCNs at *each* layer, rather than just at the input or output, is a reasonably novel contribution. This allows for a more dynamic and interactive integration of information.  The architecture is well described.
    * **Significance:** Virtual screening is a crucial area in drug discovery. Demonstrating a significant improvement in classification accuracy, even a few percentage points, can have a tangible impact on the efficiency of the process.  The reported improvement in F1-score and AUC-ROC over strong baselines lends credence to the method's potential.
    * **Experimental Rigor:** The paper includes experiments across several datasets, ensuring a more robust evaluation. The authors also detail the hyperparameter optimization process for all models, promoting fair comparison. The use of established datasets (e.g., from ChEMBL) and standard evaluation metrics (accuracy, F1-score, AUC-ROC) makes the results readily comparable to other work in the field.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the architecture, experimental setup, and results.  The rationale for the design choices is well articulated.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement:**  While the results show statistically significant improvements, the *magnitude* of the improvement, while valuable, is somewhat incremental. The F1-score improvement is only a few percentage points over the baseline GCN. Whether this translates to a substantial real-world advantage in identifying drug candidates requires further investigation.
    * **Computational Overhead:** The authors acknowledge the computational overhead of LLM-based SMILES embedding. This is a significant practical concern, especially in large-scale virtual screening applications. While they propose caching embeddings, a more thorough analysis of the computational cost and scalability of the method is needed.
    * **LLM Selection and Tuning:** The paper doesn't deeply analyze the effect of different LLMs on the results. The choice of LLM seems somewhat arbitrary. Fine-tuning the LLM on the specific chemical domains of the datasets could potentially lead to further performance gains, and this aspect remains unexplored.
    * **Interpretability:** While the authors claim interpretability as an advantage, they don't provide any specific examples of how the LLM embeddings contribute to understanding model predictions.  A more detailed analysis of the learned representations and their influence on the classification outcome would be beneficial.
    * **Limited Discussion of Related Work:** The related work section is decent, but a deeper dive into hybrid approaches (e.g., combining GCNs with other kinds of knowledge graphs or chemical features) would strengthen the framing of the contribution.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * The paper could spur further research into multi-modal approaches for virtual screening, combining structural and semantic information. The specific architecture proposed may serve as a foundation for future hybrid models.
    * The emphasis on integrating global context (via LLMs) into GCNs could inspire new techniques for knowledge transfer and generalization in molecular property prediction.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to combining GCNs and LLMs for improved molecular classification.  The core idea of per-layer integration of LLM embeddings shows promise. However, the incremental nature of the performance gains, the computational overhead, and the limited exploration of LLM selection and tuning prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. A score of 6 reflects the solid novelty, experimental rigor, and clarity of presentation, while acknowledging the limitations discussed above.  Future work addressing these limitations could significantly enhance the impact of this research.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### RefVNLI: Towards Scalable Evaluation of Subject-driven Text-to-image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17502v1)
- **Authors**: Aviv Slobodkin, Hagai Taitelbaum, Yonatan Bitton, Brian Gordon, Michal Sokolik, Nitzan Bitton Guetta, Almog Gueta, Royi Rassin, Itay Laish, Dani Lischinski, Idan Szpektor
- **Abstract**: Subject-driven text-to-image (T2I) generation aims to produce images that align with a given textual description, while preserving the visual identity from a referenced subject image. Despite its broad downstream applicability -- ranging from enhanced personalization in image generation to consistent character representation in video rendering -- progress in this field is limited by the lack of reliable automatic evaluation. Existing methods either assess only one aspect of the task (i.e., textual alignment or subject preservation), misalign with human judgments, or rely on costly API-based evaluation. To address this, we introduce RefVNLI, a cost-effective metric that evaluates both textual alignment and subject preservation in a single prediction. Trained on a large-scale dataset derived from video-reasoning benchmarks and image perturbations, RefVNLI outperforms or matches existing baselines across multiple benchmarks and subject categories (e.g., \emph{Animal}, \emph{Object}), achieving up to 6.4-point gains in textual alignment and 8.5-point gains in subject consistency. It also excels with lesser-known concepts, aligning with human preferences at over 87\% accuracy.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper and evaluate its contribution.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces REFVNLI, a novel, cost-effective metric for automatically evaluating subject-driven text-to-image (T2I) generation. Unlike existing evaluation methods that either focus solely on textual alignment or subject preservation or rely on expensive API calls, REFVNLI assesses both aspects within a single framework. The authors train REFVNLI on a large-scale, automatically generated dataset derived from video-reasoning benchmarks and image perturbations.  Experiments demonstrate that REFVNLI outperforms or matches existing baselines across multiple benchmarks and subject categories, even for lesser-known concepts, while aligning well with human preferences.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its design and implementation of a *unified* and *cost-effective* automatic metric for subject-driven T2I evaluation. Existing metrics often address textual alignment *or* subject preservation separately, or involve expensive external API calls.  REFVNLI aims to provide a balanced and efficient alternative. While components of REFVNLI draw inspiration from earlier work (e.g., using a pre-trained vision-language model, fine-tuning on a specifically curated dataset), the specific combination and the automated dataset construction are significant departures. The dataset creation process, which leverages video frames and image perturbations, is a crucial element that ensures robustness to identity-agnostic variations.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from addressing a critical bottleneck in the rapidly growing field of subject-driven T2I generation: *the lack of reliable automatic evaluation*.  Subject-driven T2I has broad potential applications, including personalized image generation and consistent character representation in video content.  The absence of accurate and efficient evaluation metrics hinders progress by making it difficult to quantitatively assess the performance of different models and identify areas for improvement. REFVNLI directly tackles this issue, potentially accelerating research in the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unified Evaluation:**  REFVNLI provides a single metric encompassing both textual alignment and subject preservation, simplifying the evaluation process.
    *   **Cost-Effectiveness:** The model's reliance on a fine-tuned VLM and an automatically generated dataset makes it significantly more cost-effective than API-based alternatives like DreamBench++ and VIEScore.
    *   **Strong Performance:** Experimental results demonstrate that REFVNLI achieves competitive or superior performance compared to existing baselines across a range of benchmarks and subject categories.
    *   **Human Alignment:**  The paper highlights that REFVNLI aligns well with human preferences, particularly for rare entities, making it a more reliable metric.
    *   **Detailed Ablation:** The thorough ablation study provides valuable insights into the importance of different design choices within REFVNLI, such as the dual-binary classification scheme and the inclusion of subject markup.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Automated Dataset Generation:** While the automated dataset creation process is a strength, it also introduces a potential weakness. The quality of the training data depends on the performance of the LLMs (e.g. Gemini) and object detection models used in the pipeline. Potential biases or inaccuracies in these models could propagate into the REFVNLI metric. While the filtering process in Section 9.1 attempts to address this, it is not foolproof.
    *   **Potential Overfitting:** As the model is specifically fine-tuned on a generated dataset, it might be overfitting to certain types of artifacts or biases within that dataset. Generalizability to completely unseen data or different evaluation paradigms might be limited.
